ChatGPT:
I agree with your revised direction. AgentPort should provide event delivery, channel-use registration, and expiring activity leases—but developers should decide whether an event creates a new session, wakes an existing one, or is handled by an already-running agent.
That keeps AgentPort compatible with Naelao, OpenClaw, LangGraph, and custom agent runtimes without imposing one session architecture on everyone.
There's one important distinction: a channel being used does not necessarily mean incoming events should be suppressed. AgentPort should continue receiving and storing them. What can be suppressed or deferred is the additional wake-up.
1. The architecture

  
    
    **WhatsApp / X / Email / Other Providers**
    Incoming events
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Event Inbox**
    Durable storage · Subscriptions · Delivery cursor
  
  
  
    
    **Activity Lease Registry**
    Which agent runs are actively consuming which ports?
  
  
  
    
      
        **Reuse active run**
        Notify it of pending events
      
    
    
      
        **Start another run**
        If developer policy allows
      
    
  


The registry does not own or manage the agent's sessions. It only tracks temporary activity claims that runtimes voluntarily register.
2. Registering channel usage
When an agent begins actively handling WhatsApp, its runtime can register a lease.
const lease = await agentport.activity.acquire({
  agentId: "agt_123",
  portId: "whatsapp_456",
  consumerId: "naelao:session_789",
  ttlSeconds: 60
});

While the session is running, Naelao renews the lease periodically:
await agentport.activity.renew({
  leaseId: lease.id,
  ttlSeconds: 60
});

When it finishes:
await agentport.activity.release({
  leaseId: lease.id
});

These are proposed APIs.
If the runtime crashes, stops renewing, or loses connectivity, the lease expires automatically. The port is no longer marked as actively consumed.
This is better than a permanent isOpen: true flag, because a crashed agent cannot leave the port permanently locked.
3. What should happen when another message arrives?
I'd offer three event-delivery policies.
{@body const [policy,setPolicy] = DIL.useState("notify")}{@body const [ttl,setTtl] = DIL.useState(60)}            AgentPort Event Delivery Policy      Example: WhatsApp connection        Design preview        When a consumer already has an active lease                  Notify existing consumer
    <text color="secondary" size="xs">Store events and notify the active runtime; avoid redundant wake-ups.</text>
  </radio>
  <radio value="queue">
    **Queue until lease expires or releases**

    <text color="secondary" size="xs">Keep incoming events pending until the current consumer finishes.</text>
  </radio>
  <radio value="parallel">
    **Allow parallel wake-ups**

    <text color="secondary" size="xs">Deliver events independently to subscribed consumers.</text>
  </radio>
</radio-group>

  
  
    
      Activity lease duration
      {ttl} seconds
    
    
  
  
    Expected behavior
    {policy==="notify"?"A message arriving while the port is in use is stored and signaled to the registered consumer. A second wake-up is deferred unless the runtime requests one.":policy==="queue"?"Messages accumulate in the durable inbox. Another wake-up can be issued after the lease is released or expires.":"Multiple subscribed sessions or agents can receive event notifications concurrently, subject to permissions."} Leases expire after {ttl} seconds without renewal.
  
  GenUI.copy(JSON.stringify({activeConsumerPolicy:policy,leaseTtlSeconds:ttl},null,2))}>
     Copy configuration
  


My default would be notify existing consumer, with a short lease and automatic expiration.
However, an activity lease is not proof that the consumer is actually processing messages. The runtime still needs to acknowledge events, and AgentPort must retry unacknowledged deliveries.
4. Your multi-agent example changes one design decision
Suppose three agents share the same WhatsApp account:

Customer Support Agent
Sales Agent
Research Agent

All three might access the same channel simultaneously.
Therefore, do not place one exclusive lock on the entire channel by default.
Instead, leases should be scoped to a consumer group or subscription.
For example:
{
  portId: "whatsapp_456",
  subscriptionId: "sub_support",
  consumerGroup: "support",
  consumerId: "naelao:session_789",
  expiresAt: "..."
}

The Sales Agent can have its own subscription and lease for the same port.
You can support:



Delivery mode
Behavior



Competing consumers
One event is handled by one consumer in a group


Fan-out
Every subscribed agent receives its own event


Filtered routing
Different agents receive different conversations or event types


Manual polling
Agent reads the inbox without automatic wake-ups


This is more flexible than saying a port is either globally open or closed.
5. Separate access leases from wake-up ownership
This is a subtle but important improvement.
An agent may open a channel tool just to send one message. That shouldn't automatically block a customer-support agent from receiving incoming messages.
So I'd distinguish:
Access activity: A session is currently using a channel API.
Wake ownership: A particular consumer has claimed responsibility for processing events from a subscription or thread.
Only wake ownership should affect whether another run is started.
For example:
await agentport.activity.acquire({
  portId: "whatsapp_456",
  subscriptionId: "sub_support",
  consumerId: "run_123",
  purpose: "event_consumer",
  ttlSeconds: 60
});

If a different agent merely calls messages.send, it doesn't acquire wake ownership.
This avoids accidentally suppressing legitimate events.
6. What AgentPort should implement versus what developers implement



Feature
AgentPort
Developer/runtime



Receive channel events
Yes
No


Store pending events
Yes
Optional local copy


Register event subscriptions
Yes
Configure them


Register activity leases
Provide API
Acquire and renew


Expire stale leases
Yes
No


Choose which session runs
No
Yes


Resume or create sessions
No
Yes


Route events between agents
Provide filters and delivery groups
Define routing policy


Prevent duplicate event processing
Delivery IDs and claim mechanisms
Idempotent processing


Manage model context
No
Yes


AgentPort can provide an optional managed delivery policy, but it shouldn't require all developers to use it.
7. How this works for Naelao
I would build a small AgentPort Runtime Adapter inside Naelao.
When a Naelao run starts handling events, the adapter acquires a lease. While the run is active, it renews the lease. When the run finishes, it acknowledges processed events and releases the lease.
If Naelao crashes, AgentPort detects lease expiration and makes the unacknowledged work eligible for redelivery.
The important detail is that expiration should not automatically create unlimited new sessions. It should notify the configured runtime endpoint or make the event available for polling, respecting retry limits and backoff.
8. Minimal API surface
You could start with just these operations:
POST   /v1/events/subscriptions
GET    /v1/events
POST   /v1/events/claim
POST   /v1/events/ack

POST   /v1/activity/leases
POST   /v1/activity/leases/:id/renew
DELETE /v1/activity/leases/:id

For the MVP, I wouldn't build a complicated distributed scheduler inside AgentPort.
Let AgentPort handle durable events, subscriptions, claims, leases and delivery.
Let Naelao handle sessions, agent execution, wake scheduling and context management.
Final recommendation
Your instinct to leave session orchestration to developers is right.
AgentPort should expose the primitives that make reliable agent wake-ups possible without dictating how agents work.
The key primitives are:

Durable event inboxes.
Subscriptions and delivery modes.
Expiring activity leases.
Event claims, acknowledgments and retries.
Optional wake-up notifications.

And most importantly, a session using a channel should not automatically block other sessions or agents from using it. Only an explicitly claimed event-consumer lease should influence wake-up delivery, and only within its configured subscription or consumer group.
That gives developers complete freedom while still solving the crash-recovery and duplicate-wake-up problems you identified.


You:
We should support mcp mcpv2 API cli


ChatGPT:
Yes. AgentPort should support MCP, MCP v2, REST API, and CLI as four interfaces to the same underlying infrastructure.
But I would make one architectural decision: none of these interfaces should implement its own business logic. They should all call the same AgentPort Core, with identical identity, permissions, billing, and event behavior.
That way, an agent using the CLI has exactly the same capabilities and security restrictions as an agent using MCP or the API.
Proposed AgentPort interface architecture

  
    {#each [{i:"plug",t:"MCP",s:"Standard agent tools"},{i:"workflow",t:"MCP v2",s:"Enhanced AgentPort interface"},{i:"code",t:"REST API",s:"SDKs and integrations"},{i:"terminal",t:"CLI",s:"Shell and coding agents"}] as x}
      
        
          
          **{x.t}**
          {x.s}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    
      **AgentPort Core**
      Identity · Providers · Accounts · Events · Leases · Trust · Billing
    
    
      {#each ["Identity Registry","Provider Router","Policy Engine","Event Bus","Credential Vault","Usage Metering"] as x}
        
          
            {x}
          
        
      {/each}
    
  
  
  
    **Provider Adapters**
    AgentPort Mail · WhatsApp · Crossmint · Coinbase · Stripe · Third-party providers

Yes. AgentPort should support MCP, MCP v2, REST API, and CLI as four interfaces to the same underlying infrastructure.
But I would make one architectural decision: none of these interfaces should implement its own business logic. They should all call the same AgentPort Core, with identical identity, permissions, billing, and event behavior.
That way, an agent using the CLI has exactly the same capabilities and security restrictions as an agent using MCP or the API.
Proposed AgentPort interface architecture

  
    {#each [{i:"plug",t:"MCP",s:"Standard agent tools"},{i:"workflow",t:"MCP v2",s:"Enhanced AgentPort interface"},{i:"code",t:"REST API",s:"SDKs and integrations"},{i:"terminal",t:"CLI",s:"Shell and coding agents"}] as x}
      
        
          
          **{x.t}**
          {x.s}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    
      **AgentPort Core**
      Identity · Providers · Accounts · Events · Leases · Trust · Billing
    
    
      {#each ["Identity Registry","Provider Router","Policy Engine","Event Bus","Credential Vault","Usage Metering"] as x}
        
          
            {x}
          
        
      {/each}
    
  
  
  
    **Provider Adapters**
    AgentPort Mail · WhatsApp · Crossmint · Coinbase · Stripe · Third-party providers
  


Important clarification about MCP v2
The official MCP specification currently uses date-based versions, with 2026-07-28 being the latest published revision I found—not an officially named “MCP v2.”
That revision introduced a stateless protocol core, revised subscriptions, improved authorization, and an extensions framework. <Cite refs={["turn972818search1","turn972818search7"]}/>
I would therefore support:



Interface
AgentPort endpoint
Purpose



MCP (legacy compatibility)
mcp.agentport.naelao.com
Existing MCP clients


MCP latest
Same endpoint, protocol negotiation
Modern MCP clients


REST API
api.agentport.naelao.com/v1
Complete programmable access


CLI
agentport ...
Terminal and coding agents


If by MCP v2 you mean an AgentPort-specific advanced interface, I'd implement it using standard MCP extensions rather than creating an incompatible protocol.
What each interface exposes
For example, the same event subscription could be created through three different interfaces.
REST API
POST /v1/events/subscriptions
Authorization: Bearer <token>
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "agentId": "agt_123",
  "portId": "whatsapp_456",
  "event": "message.received",
  "delivery": "webhook"
}

CLI
agentport events subscribe \
  --agent agt_123 \
  --port whatsapp_456 \
  --event message.received

MCP tool
agentport_events_subscribe

All three call the same internal EventSubscriptionService, returning the same subscription ID.
For the complete product, expose identities, providers, account provisioning, messages, events, activity leases, KYA, delegations, and approvals through these interfaces, subject to each client's permissions.
One thing I'd do differently for events
MCP's newest specification supports subscriptions, but AgentPort's durable event inbox should remain independent of MCP connections. The protocol's subscription streams are not a substitute for your own persisted event delivery, acknowledgment, retries, and lease expiration. 
That lets a developer subscribe through MCP, receive events through a webhook, and process them with a CLI-based agent without losing messages when a connection closes.
Implementation priority
I'd build in this order:

REST API + shared AgentPort Core — the foundation.
CLI — especially useful for Naelao coding agents and OpenClaw-style runtimes.
MCP server — support the latest specification and older clients where necessary.
TypeScript and Python SDKs — typed wrappers over the REST API.
Provider SDK — allow institutions to publish provisioning, tools, and events into AgentPort.

The result is that AgentPort becomes accessible to almost any agent architecture without requiring developers to change their session or memory systems.
One core, multiple interfaces, no duplicated permission logic.

ChatGPT:
What else AgentPort should have
Given what we've designed so far, I think AgentPort should become an operating infrastructure layer for autonomous AI agents, not just an identity service, channel provider, or collection of MCP tools.
Its purpose should be to let any AI agent securely acquire identities, connect to services, receive events, interact with people, perform authorized actions, and operate reliably across different runtimes.
You already have the foundations: agent identities, UserPort, channel provisioning, KYA, provider integrations, event subscriptions, activity leases, MCP, API, and CLI.
The next features should fill the gaps between an agent having access to a service and an agent being able to operate independently, securely, and economically.

    AgentPort
    Universal agent identity, connectivity and action infrastructure

The next features should fill the gaps between an agent having access to a service and an agent being able to operate independently, securely, and economically.

2. The additional features I would prioritize



Feature
What it enables
Priority



Credential Vault
Securely store OAuth tokens, API keys and provider credentials without exposing them to agents
P0


Agent Capability Registry
Discover what each agent can do and which providers it can access
P0


Delegation Engine
Give agents temporary, scoped authority to act for users, companies or other agents
P0


Action Approval System
Require human approval for purchases, transfers, posting or sensitive account changes
P0


Usage & Budget Engine
Set limits per agent, provider, action and time period
P0


Event Replay & Dead-Letter Queue
Recover failed deliveries, retry events and investigate errors
P0


Agent Observability
Inspect tool calls, event deliveries, costs, failures and security decisions
P1


Provider Health & Failover
Detect outages, expired credentials and alternate providers
P1


Agent-to-Agent Communication
Allow authorized agents to discover, message and delegate tasks to one another
P1


Agent Service Directory
Publish agents and services for other agents to discover
P2


Portable State & Checkpoints
Transfer limited task state between runtimes without moving entire conversation histories
P2


Reputation & Attestations
Record verified credentials, operational history and institution-issued claims
P2


P0 means core infrastructure, P1 means after the first working product, and P2 means later expansion.
3. Three features that could make AgentPort particularly valuable
A. Agent Capability Passport
Every agent gets a machine-readable record of what it is authorized to do.
{
  "agentId": "agt_123",
  "owner": "org_456",
  "capabilities": [
    "email.send",
    "whatsapp.read",
    "wallet.pay"
  ],
  "limits": {
    "wallet.pay": {
      "maxAmountUSD": 20,
      "dailyBudgetUSD": 100
    }
  },
  "verification": "organization_verified"
}

This would be a discovery and policy representation, not a bearer credential. Providers still need to enforce the underlying authorization and verify signed claims where applicable.
Imagine a bank, SaaS company or another agent asking AgentPort:
"Who operates this agent, what is it verified to do, and what permissions does it actually have?"
That's more valuable than a simple agent ID.
B. Agent-to-Agent Delegation
Imagine a research agent hiring a data-collection agent.

  
    
    **Research Agent**
    Owns research task and budget
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Delegation Contract**
    Task scope · Spending cap · Expiration · Revocation
  
  
  
    
      
        
        **Data Agent**
        Collect evidence
      
    
    
      
        
        **Analysis Agent**
        Analyze results
      
    
  


AgentPort manages who can delegate, what authority can be passed on, and how to revoke it.
Naelao or another runtime handles the actual agent reasoning and task execution.
C. AgentPort Action Receipts
This is one of the features I'd strongly consider building early.
Whenever an agent performs an important action, AgentPort issues an immutable or tamper-evident record describing:

Which agent initiated the action.
Which owner or organization authorized it.
Which provider executed it.
What permission and approval were used.
Whether the action succeeded, failed or was reversed.

For example, an agent sends a payment, publishes a social media post or provisions a new account.
The owner should be able to inspect the action later and see exactly what happened. A receipt is evidence of the recorded operation, not proof that every underlying statement or external outcome is true.

      AgentPort Console
      Illustrative agent overview
    
    Connected

          {x.n}
          {x.l}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
    Recent activity
    {#each [{icon:"message-square",t:"WhatsApp message received",s:"Event queued · 2 seconds ago",c:"success"},{icon:"key-round",t:"Access token refreshed",s:"Gmail connection · 4 minutes ago",c:"secondary"},{icon:"shield-check",t:"Payment approval required",s:"Wallet action · Awaiting owner",c:"caution"},{icon:"timer",t:"Activity lease expired",s:"Runtime recovered · 12 minutes ago",c:"secondary"}] as x}
      
        
          
        
        
          {x.t}
          {x.s}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  Sample data, not live AgentPort activity.


Developers should be able to inspect event delivery, leases, authentication failures, provider connections, usage and costs from one console.
For tracing, I'd use OpenTelemetry rather than inventing a proprietary format. Its GenAI conventions already cover agent invocations, workflows and tool executions, although parts remain under development. <Cite refs={["turn231959search1","turn231959search12"]}/>
5. What AgentPort should not become
This is equally important.



Keep inside AgentPort
Leave to Naelao or other runtimes



Agent identity and credentials
Model inference


Account provisioning
Agent reasoning


Channel access and events
Conversation memory


Action permissions
Planning and workflows


Delegation and approvals
Agent session orchestration


Usage limits and audit
Prompt construction


Provider marketplace
Full app/website building


I would avoid building another LangGraph, n8n, OpenClaw or model-hosting platform inside AgentPort.
Instead, AgentPort should make those systems more capable.
6. The product roadmap I would follow

  
    
      
    
    
      **Phase 1 — Agent connectivity**

  Identity, authentication, credential vault, channel provisioning, MCP/API/CLI, subscriptions, event inboxes and activity leases.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Goal: an external agent can register, connect WhatsApp or email, receive events and respond.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Phase 2 — Trust and controlled actions**

  Delegation, approvals, KYA, budget policies, audit receipts, provider SDK and institution integrations.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Goal: institutions can confidently grant limited access to agents.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Phase 3 — Agent economy**

  Agent discovery, agent-to-agent delegation, wallet integrations, paid services, provider marketplace and verifiable reputation.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Goal: agents can collaborate and transact through authorized services.</text>
</box>

  


7. One more architectural feature: a universal AgentPort manifest
I'd give every registered agent a discoverable manifest.
{
  "agentportVersion": "1",
  "agentId": "agt_123",
  "runtime": {
    "type": "external",
    "framework": "custom"
  },
  "interfaces": ["mcp", "api", "cli"],
  "eventDelivery": {
    "type": "webhook"
  },
  "capabilities": [
    "channels",
    "events",
    "identity",
    "delegation"
  ]
}

It could help external developers integrate AgentPort without adopting Naelao. The manifest describes supported interfaces and capabilities; actual authorization must be checked separately.
MCP already provides protocol negotiation and extension mechanisms, so use those standards where possible instead of creating unnecessary competing protocols. 

My recommendation for your next milestone
Don't attempt all ten infrastructure areas at once.
Build one compelling end-to-end demonstration:
A developer registers an existing OpenClaw or custom agent with AgentPort, provisions an email address, connects WhatsApp, subscribes to incoming events, receives wake-up notifications, replies using MCP or CLI, and can inspect every action from the AgentPort dashboard.
The runtime remains completely independent of Naelao.
That demonstration would prove AgentPort's central value: agents can acquire persistent identities, communicate with the outside world, and operate through a common infrastructure layer without changing their underlying intelligence or runtime.
Once that works reliably, I'd prioritize delegation, approvals and institutional integrations. Those are the features that could make AgentPort harder to replace than a simple collection of provider adapters.

You:
Mmm I think that's too much like for a2a there can be many connectors that have a2a and agents can use agentport to regester so we are not trying to create adaptors/connectors


ChatGPT:
I agree. That changes AgentPort's scope in an important way.
AgentPort shouldn't become another integration platform, agent framework, or A2A network. It should be the infrastructure that agents use to register, access, and manage external services through existing providers and connectors.
For example, if an A2A connector already exists, AgentPort doesn't need to implement A2A. An agent should be able to register that connector with AgentPort and use it through MCP, API, or CLI.
A simpler AgentPort architecture

  
    
    **Any Agent**
    Naelao · OpenClaw · LangGraph · Custom runtimes
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Register · Discover · Authorize · Access · Subscribe
    
      {#each ["Agent identity","Registered services","Credential references","Events & leases"] as x}
        
          
            {x}
          
        
      {/each}
    
  
  
  
    {#each [{i:"plug",n:"Existing MCP Servers"},{i:"network",n:"Existing A2A Services"},{i:"globe",n:"External APIs"},{i:"message-circle",n:"Channel Providers"}] as x}
      
        
          
          {x.n}
        
      
    {/each}
  


What AgentPort actually needs
I would reduce the core to six responsibilities:



Core
Responsibility



1. Agent registration
Persistent identity and owner association


2. Service registration
Register existing connectors, MCP servers, A2A endpoints, APIs and accounts


3. Access management
Credentials, ownership, scopes and permissions


4. Unified access
MCP, API, CLI and SDK interfaces


5. Event subscriptions
Register events, receive notifications and deliver them to agents


6. Activity leases
Optional heartbeat and expiration for consumers handling events


Everything else should be implemented by existing providers, connectors, or agent runtimes.
Example: A2A without building A2A
Suppose an agent already uses an A2A service.
The developer registers it:
agentport services register \
  --name research-network \
  --type a2a \
  --endpoint https://example.com/a2a

AgentPort stores the service registration and its authentication configuration.
Then the agent can discover it:
agentport services list

And access it through its existing A2A client, or through an AgentPort-managed proxy if that service supports the required integration.
AgentPort doesn't implement A2A messaging, task delegation, or agent discovery itself. It simply makes the registered service available under the agent's permissions.
The same principle applies to email, WhatsApp, payments and other providers. Use existing services wherever possible rather than creating your own adapter for every provider.
One caveat: a service registration alone doesn't make an arbitrary external API compatible with MCP or CLI. AgentPort can expose connection details and credentials securely, but actual invocation requires a compatible client, an existing connector, or an explicitly configured proxy.
What about event triggers?
Keep your existing approach.
A provider or connector declares the events it supports. AgentPort stores subscriptions and forwards notifications to the developer's runtime.
For example:
await agentport.services.register({
  name: "research-network",
  type: "a2a",
  endpoint: "https://example.com/a2a",
  events: {
    source: "https://example.com/events",
    delivery: "webhook"
  }
});

This describes an existing event source; AgentPort doesn't invent A2A event behavior.
For connectors without push notifications, the developer can use polling or an external scheduler.
The most important product boundary
AgentPort is not where agents think, collaborate, or execute workflows. It's where they register and manage their access to the external world.
That gives you a much smaller product to build and maintain.
I'd make agent identity + service registration + credential access + event subscriptions the MVP. Add leases only where developers need them for reliable event handling.
And I would avoid building an AgentPort-native A2A system, workflow engine, marketplace, or proprietary connector ecosystem until actual developer demand proves they're necessary.
This makes AgentPort a neutral infrastructure layer that can work with the connectors developers already use.


You:
What about since it has access to all intergrations under one port won't it become too big for an agent to handle


ChatGPT:
Yes. If AgentPort exposes every integration and every tool to an agent at once, it will become too large for the agent to use efficiently. This is especially problematic for inexpensive models with limited context windows.
But the solution isn't to split AgentPort into hundreds of separate services. It's to make AgentPort a single entry point with dynamic capability discovery and loading.
1. One Port, thousands of capabilities, only a few loaded

  
    
    **Agent**
    Only sees a small AgentPort interface
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Discovery Layer**
    
      {#each ["Search capabilities","Inspect capability","Execute action","Manage subscriptions"] as x}
        
          
            {x}
          
        
      {/each}
    
  
  
  
    **Registered Services**
    Only relevant integrations are retrieved and used
    
      {#each ["Gmail","WhatsApp","Stripe","A2A","GitHub","Other"] as x}
        
          
            {x}
          
        
      {/each}
    
  


The agent doesn't need to know every capability in advance.
It only needs to know how to find and invoke capabilities.
2. I'd expose five core tools
For MCP, start with a small fixed interface:



Tool
Purpose



agentport_search
Find relevant registered services and capabilities


agentport_inspect
Retrieve a capability's input schema, permissions and description


agentport_execute
Execute an authorized capability


agentport_events
Read notifications and manage event subscriptions


agentport_connections
List or manage connected accounts


For example, the user says:

"Find the latest email from my supplier and send them a WhatsApp reply."

The agent first searches:
{
  "query": "search my email and send WhatsApp message"
}

AgentPort might return only:
{
  "capabilities": [
    {
      "id": "gmail.messages.search",
      "description": "Search connected Gmail"
    },
    {
      "id": "whatsapp.messages.send",
      "description": "Send WhatsApp message"
    }
  ]
}

The agent inspects the relevant schemas and invokes them.
This avoids loading hundreds of unrelated tool definitions into the model's context.
3. But there's a tradeoff with generic execute tools
A single agentport_execute tool is convenient, but it has disadvantages.
The model must supply an action identifier and arguments correctly. If the tool schema is too generic, smaller models may make more mistakes.
So I'd support two execution modes.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Dynamic discovery**

  The agent searches, inspects, and executes capabilities through a small fixed interface. Best for large catalogs and low-context models.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Scoped native tools**

  AgentPort exposes only the tools belonging to selected integrations, with their original typed schemas. Best for frequently used tools and runtimes that support dynamic tool registration.
</box>

  


For example, an email agent might permanently load five Gmail tools while discovering everything else on demand.
4. The CLI can be even more efficient
Coding agents already know how to use a terminal.
Rather than loading hundreds of MCP tools, give them one CLI entry point:
agentport search "send WhatsApp message"

agentport inspect whatsapp.messages.send

agentport execute whatsapp.messages.send \
  --args '{"to":"+263...","text":"Hello"}'

The CLI could also provide short, human-readable documentation:
agentport help whatsapp

This can reduce the number of tool definitions exposed to the model, although command output and schemas still consume tokens.
5. Add context-aware capability loading
AgentPort can prioritize tools using the current task, the agent's permissions, and recent usage.
For example:
{@body const [task,setTask] = DIL.useState("Reply to customer emails and notify them on WhatsApp")}{@body const capabilities = [{id:"gmail.search",name:"Gmail · Search messages",terms:["email","mail","inbox","customer","supplier"]},{id:"gmail.reply",name:"Gmail · Reply",terms:["reply","email","mail"]},{id:"whatsapp.send",name:"WhatsApp · Send message",terms:["whatsapp","notify","message"]},{id:"github.issues",name:"GitHub · Issues",terms:["github","issue","code"]},{id:"stripe.payment",name:"Stripe · Payments",terms:["payment","charge","money","invoice"]},{id:"a2a.send",name:"A2A · Send task",terms:["agent","delegate","a2a"]}]}{@body const words = task.toLowerCase()}{@body const matched = capabilities.map(c=>({...c,score:c.terms.filter(t=>words.includes(t)).length})).filter(c=>c.score>0).sort((a,b)=>b.score-a.score)}  Capability discovery preview      Agent's current task    <br>  </box><br>  <box gap={2}><br>    <row justify="between" align="center"><br>      <text weight="medium" size="sm">Relevant capabilities</text><br>      <badge>{matched.length} matched</badge><br>    </row><br>    {#each matched as c}<br>      <row key={c.id} align="center" gap={2}><br>        <icon name="check-circle" color="success"/><br>        <box flex="1"><br>          <text size="sm">{c.name}</text><br>        </box><br>        <text color="secondary" size="xs">{c.id}</text><br>      </row><br>    {/each}<br>    {#if matched.length===0}<br>      <text color="secondary" size="sm">No matching capabilities. Try describing another task.</text><br>    {/if}<br>  </box><br>  <caption>Illustrative keyword matching only. Production discovery should also check permissions, schemas and service availability.</caption><br></box></p>
<h2>6. Don't make AgentPort responsible for the agent's context window</h2>
<p>AgentPort should provide compact discovery results, pagination, filtering, and optional schema loading.</p>
<p>But the agent runtime should decide how many tools to load and when to remove them.</p>
<p>That preserves your goal of supporting OpenClaw, Naelao and other agent builders without forcing them to change their architecture.</p>
<h2>7. My recommended final interface</h2>
<p>For AgentPort, I'd expose a small universal interface plus optional native capabilities:</p>
<pre><code class="language-text">agentport
├── search
├── inspect
├── execute
├── events
├── connections
└── tools
    └── [dynamically loaded native tools]
</code></pre>
<p>One important security requirement: <code>execute</code> must validate the arguments against the registered capability schema and enforce authorization server-side. The model should never be able to invoke an arbitrary URL or access a secret just by supplying a capability ID.</p>
<p><strong>So yes, AgentPort can have thousands of integrations behind one port without overwhelming an agent. The agent only needs a small discovery interface and access to the handful of capabilities relevant to its current task.</strong></p>
<p>I'd make dynamic capability discovery one of AgentPort's core features from the beginning.</p>


You:
The biggest hurdles will be when we are at the stage of talking to online services lol Amazon trying to ask them for agents to be able to create their own Amazon accounts because how would we convince them to write code for that and why us etc plus not just amazon many services


ChatGPT:
That's probably one of AgentPort's biggest business challenges, and it may be harder than building the infrastructure.
Imagine approaching Amazon, Google, Meta, banks, telecom operators, or thousands of SaaS companies and saying:

"We're building infrastructure that lets AI agents create their own accounts on your platform. Can you modify your systems to support us?"

Their immediate questions would likely be:

Why should we trust these agents?
Who is legally responsible when an agent commits fraud or violates our terms?
Why should we spend engineering resources integrating with AgentPort?
How many customers or transactions will you bring us?
Why shouldn't we build this ourselves?

And the biggest question: Why should we integrate with AgentPort instead of directly supporting OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or another large platform?
I think the solution is to change what you're asking them to do.
1. Don't initially ask companies to create accounts for agents
Instead, offer them three levels of participation.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 1 — Existing access, zero provider engineering**

  AgentPort registers a provider's existing OAuth, MCP, A2A, or API integration and uses it within the provider's permitted rules.

  An agent acts through an authorized human or company account. The provider doesn't need to know about AgentPort initially.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 2 — Agent-aware authorization**

  The provider accepts verified agent identities, owner/delegation information, and scoped permissions from AgentPort.

  This requires some integration, but the provider doesn't need to build an entirely new account system.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 3 — Agent-native accounts**

  The provider explicitly supports creating accounts or subaccounts for agents, with eligibility, billing, ownership, identity verification, and lifecycle management.

  This is the long-term goal, not the first partnership request.
</box>

  


An important caveat: registering an existing connector doesn't automatically give AgentPort permission to provision new accounts or resell access. Each provider's API permissions and terms still apply.


ChatGPT:
That's probably one of AgentPort's biggest business challenges, and it may be harder than building the infrastructure.
Imagine approaching Amazon, Google, Meta, banks, telecom operators, or thousands of SaaS companies and saying:

How many customers or transactions will you bring us?
Why shouldn't we build this ourselves?

And the biggest question: Why should we integrate with AgentPort instead of directly supporting OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or another large platform?
I think the solution is to change what you're asking them to do.
1. Don't initially ask companies to create accounts for agents
Instead, offer them three levels of participation.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 1 — Existing access, zero provider engineering**

  AgentPort registers a provider's existing OAuth, MCP, A2A, or API integration and uses it within the provider's permitted rules.

  An agent acts through an authorized human or company account. The provider doesn't need to know about AgentPort initially.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 2 — Agent-aware authorization**

  The provider accepts verified agent identities, owner/delegation information, and scoped permissions from AgentPort.

  This requires some integration, but the provider doesn't need to build an entirely new account system.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Level 3 — Agent-native accounts**

  The provider explicitly supports creating accounts or subaccounts for agents, with eligibility, billing, ownership, identity verification, and lifecycle management.

  This is the long-term goal, not the first partnership request.
</box>

  


An important caveat: registering an existing connector doesn't automatically give AgentPort permission to provision new accounts or resell access. Each provider's API permissions and terms still apply.
2. What would actually convince Amazon or another large company?
The strongest argument isn't "AI agents are the future."
It's new customers, additional revenue, lower integration costs, and manageable risk.
For example, instead of pitching Amazon:

"Please build an API so every AI agent can register its own Amazon account."

Eventually, you want to pitch something closer to:

"AgentPort connects independently operated AI agents to online services. We can provide verified ownership, delegated authorization, usage controls, and audit records. You retain control over which agents can access your service and what they can do. We want to explore an approved agent-account or subaccount integration that brings additional business to Amazon."

That's a much easier conversation once you have usage data.
Amazon already provides a useful starting point
Amazon's Selling Partner API lets approved applications act on behalf of authorized sellers. That is delegated access to existing seller accounts, not permission to create independent Amazon accounts for agents. <Cite refs={["turn581941search1","turn581941search2"]}/>
This distinction matters because you can prove useful agent activity before Amazon agrees to introduce a new account type.
3. The real problem is the chicken-and-egg effect

  
    
      
        **Developers**
        Want more services before adopting AgentPort
      
    
    
      
        **Providers**
        Want agent demand before integrating AgentPort
      
    
  
  
  
    **Your initial advantage**
    Existing connectors + developer adoption + measurable demand
  


You solve this by making AgentPort useful without requiring any large provider to partner with you first.
Let developers register their existing authorized connectors and MCP servers. Let agents use AgentPort identity, credentials, event subscriptions, and service discovery.
Then collect aggregated, privacy-preserving evidence of demand.
For example, imagine eventually being able to show a provider:

  Illustrative partnership metrics
  
    {#each [{v:"25k",l:"Registered agents"},{v:"8k",l:"Agents requesting service"},{v:"$2M",l:"Potential annual transaction volume"}] as x}
      
        
          {x.v}
          {x.l}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  Hypothetical figures showing the type of evidence a provider might care about, not AgentPort's actual metrics.


Now you aren't asking Amazon to bet on an unknown startup's idea. You're offering access to measurable demand.
4. I would approach smaller providers before Amazon
Smaller SaaS companies, agent-friendly infrastructure companies, and developer-tool providers may be more willing to experiment.
Your first partner might only need to publish an existing MCP endpoint, configure OAuth, and declare which account-provisioning actions are permitted.
If it already supports API-based subaccount creation, the integration could be relatively small.
For larger providers, agent-native accounts may require substantial changes to fraud detection, account recovery, billing, identity verification, support, and legal terms. That's not something a simple SDK can solve.
5. Give providers a reason to integrate once, not individually with every agent platform
This is where AgentPort could eventually have leverage.

  
    **Amazon / Bank / SaaS Provider**
    One AgentPort integration
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Identity · Delegation · Access · Events
  
  
  
    {#each ["Naelao","OpenClaw","Other runtimes"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  


The promise is that a provider can reach many independent agent developers without building separate integrations for every framework.
Of course, this only becomes convincing once those developers actually use AgentPort.
6. What I'd build now to prepare for future partnerships
Add a Provider Support Registry with explicit capabilities and restrictions.
For example:
{
  "provider": "example-commerce",
  "integration": "existing-mcp",
  "capabilities": {
    "connectExistingAccount": true,
    "createAgentAccount": false,
    "createSubaccount": false,
    "agentDelegatedAccess": true
  },
  "partnershipStatus": "unaffiliated"
}

This avoids implying that AgentPort can create accounts on services that haven't authorized it.
It also lets you measure which missing capabilities developers request most often.
Over time, those requests become evidence for partnership conversations.
7. The longer-term strategy
I see three stages:
Stage 1: Developers adopt AgentPort because it makes existing integrations easier to manage.
Stage 2: Providers begin recognizing AgentPort identities and delegated permissions because developers are already using them.
Stage 3: Providers introduce agent-native account provisioning where there is enough demand and a workable compliance model.
Stage 3 is the ambitious part. It may take years, and some providers may never support independent agent accounts.
That's why AgentPort must be valuable even if Amazon never agrees.
The key business insight is that you don't need to convince Amazon to integrate with AgentPort today. You need to build something that eventually makes Amazon and other providers see a commercial reason to support agents through AgentPort.
And I would be careful not to pitch AgentPort as replacing their account systems. The more compelling position is that AgentPort brings verified, authorized agent demand to their existing services while leaving providers in control.

So initially for accounts that don't support agent accounts should we just be like composio or just use composio but now we are using someone else s product


ChatGPT:
Yes. Level 1 is essentially Composio's territory, and that exposes an important strategic risk for AgentPort.
If AgentPort becomes nothing more than a wrapper around Composio, developers could reasonably ask:

Why pay AgentPort when I can connect my agent directly to Composio?

I wouldn't try to beat Composio by rebuilding hundreds of integrations. But I also wouldn't make AgentPort entirely dependent on Composio.
I'd separate two things:

Integration access: How an agent connects to Gmail, Amazon, GitHub, Stripe, and other services.
Agent-native identity and accounts: How an agent gets its own authorized identity, accounts, permissions, and event subscriptions.

Composio or other existing connectors can solve much of the first problem. AgentPort should concentrate on the second.
And you're asking the right question about Amazon: can an existing seller authorize an agent to operate under their seller account, perhaps using some form of subaccount, without Amazon having to introduce entirely new agent accounts?

ChatGPT:
Yes. Level 1 is essentially Composio's territory, and that exposes an important strategic risk for AgentPort.
If AgentPort becomes nothing more than a wrapper around Composio, developers could reasonably ask:

Why pay AgentPort when I can connect my agent directly to Composio?

I wouldn't try to beat Composio by rebuilding hundreds of integrations. But I also wouldn't make AgentPort entirely dependent on Composio.
I'd separate two things:

Integration access: How an agent connects to Gmail, Amazon, GitHub, Stripe, and other services.
Agent-native identity and accounts: How an agent gets its own authorized identity, accounts, permissions, and event subscriptions.

Composio or other existing connectors can solve much of the first problem. AgentPort should concentrate on the second.
And you're asking the right question about Amazon: can an existing seller authorize an agent to operate under their seller account, perhaps using some form of subaccount, without Amazon having to introduce entirely new agent accounts?
That is worth investigating before approaching Amazon for a new integration.
1. Amazon: there is already a partial path
I checked Amazon's documentation, and there's a useful distinction.



Capability
Amazon support



Seller authorizes an application via OAuth
 Yes


Application manages permitted seller operations
 Yes


Seller creates secondary users with limited permissions
 Yes, through Seller Central


Seller authorizes supported AI agents/plugins
 Yes, subject to Amazon's controls


SP-API creates independent agent seller accounts
 Not documented


SP-API creates agent-owned secondary user accounts
 Not documented


Amazon's SP-API supports seller-authorized applications with defined roles. Seller Central also has secondary-user permissions, and Amazon documents a Manage Agents interface for supported agent integrations. <Cite refs={["turn748801search1","turn748801search3","turn748801search4"]}/>
So you could initially support this model:

  
    
    **Existing Amazon Seller Account**
    Human or business owns the account
  
  Amazon-authorized application access
  
    **AgentPort**
    Registers agent identity, authorized connection and permissions
  
  
  
    
      
        **Inventory Agent**
        Manage listings
      
    
    
      
        **Order Agent**
        Process orders
      
    
  


These would be two AgentPort identities using appropriately authorized access to one seller account, not two Amazon accounts.
You can enforce separate permissions inside AgentPort, but Amazon may still see actions as coming from the same authorized application. Your internal separation must not be presented as provider-issued subaccounts.
Also, an AgentPort commercial integration serving multiple sellers would need to meet Amazon's public-application registration, authorization, and approval requirements. 
What I'd eventually ask Amazon
Not "Can we create thousands of seller accounts for bots?"
Instead:
"Can sellers create explicitly authorized, individually identifiable agent principals under their existing seller account, with scoped permissions, revocation and audit trails?"
That is a more focused product request.
It lets Amazon preserve seller accountability while enabling agents to have their own recognized operational identities.
2. Should AgentPort use Composio?
I'd use Composio initially as an optional integration backend, not as AgentPort's foundation.
Composio already offers connected accounts, hosted OAuth authorization, token management, tool discovery and event triggers. Rebuilding all of that would consume significant engineering effort. <Cite refs={["turn748801search5","turn748801search6","turn748801search7"]}/>
But you should own the AgentPort abstraction.

  
    **Agent / Developer**
    One AgentPort interface
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Core**
    Identity · Registered connections · Policies · Events
  
  
  
    {#each ["Composio","Direct OAuth / API","Existing MCP server","Provider-native agent account"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  


A developer should be able to register an existing Composio connection, use a direct OAuth connection, or connect an MCP server.
AgentPort stores its own stable connection ID and metadata, while keeping track of which backend actually holds the credentials.
Be careful about portability: Composio's managed credentials are not generally exportable, so switching backends may require users to reauthorize their accounts. 
3. But wouldn't that make AgentPort another Composio?
Only if you make generic OAuth integrations the main product.
Here's how I'd distinguish them:



Composio
AgentPort's intended focus



Connect users to existing applications
Register agents as persistent principals


Manage OAuth and API credentials
Bind authorized connections to agent identities


Discover and execute application tools
Discover registered services and their account capabilities


Provide application event triggers
Expose events with optional activity leases


Primarily act through existing accounts
Support both delegated access and provider-issued agent accounts


There is overlap. That's fine.
The distinction must become real through the features you build, not just marketing language.
For example, an AgentPort developer should eventually be able to ask:

"Can this agent create its own account with this provider, or must a human authorize an existing account?"

And get a clear answer.
4. Make account provisioning a first-class capability
I would give each registered service an account model.
type AccountModel =
  | "delegated"
  | "provider_subaccount"
  | "agent_native";

interface RegisteredService {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  accountModels: AccountModel[];
  connectionBackend: string;
}

For example:



Service
Account model



Amazon SP-API
Delegated seller authorization


Gmail OAuth
Delegated Google account


Existing Composio connector
Delegated user account


Provider with approved agent subaccounts
Provider subaccount


Provider offering agent-native registration
Agent-native account


These are different trust and ownership models, even if all of them are accessed through AgentPort.
5. What I would build first
I wouldn't build a competing library of 500 integrations.
I'd build:

Agent registration and identity.
A registry for existing MCP, API and Composio connections.
Explicit support for delegated versus agent-native accounts.
Event subscriptions and activity leases.
MCP, REST API and CLI access to those registered services.

Then choose one provider with genuine agent-account provisioning and make that experience work end to end.
That provider doesn't need to be Amazon.
In fact, a smaller provider willing to support agent identities could be more valuable initially than spending months trying to negotiate with Amazon.
The business risk to watch
If Composio powers 90% of AgentPort's usefulness, then Composio controls a critical dependency, potentially including costs, supported services, credential custody and terms.
That's acceptable for an MVP, but dangerous as a permanent dependency.
I would support bring-your-own Composio, direct connections and other compatible integration backends from the beginning, while keeping AgentPort's canonical identity and service registry independent.
My recommendation: use Composio where it saves time, but build AgentPort around something Composio doesn't automatically give you—a persistent agent identity that can progressively move from using someone else's account to holding its own provider-recognized accounts.
That's the direction that could eventually justify asking Amazon and other services to integrate directly with AgentPort.

Instead:
"Can sellers create explicitly authorized, individually identifiable agent principals under their existing seller account, with scoped permissions, revocation and audit trails?"
That is a more focused product request.
It lets Amazon preserve seller accountability while enabling agents to have their own recognized operational identities.
2. Should AgentPort use Composio?
I'd use Composio initially as an optional integration backend, not as AgentPort's foundation.
Composio already offers connected accounts, hosted OAuth authorization, token management, tool discovery and event triggers. Rebuilding all of that would consume significant engineering effort. <Cite refs={["turn748801search5","turn748801search6","turn748801search7"]}/>
But you should own the AgentPort abstraction.

  
    **Agent / Developer**
    One AgentPort interface
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Core**
    Identity · Registered connections · Policies · Events
  
  
  
    {#each ["Composio","Direct OAuth / API","Existing MCP server","Provider-native agent account"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  


A developer should be able to register an existing Composio connection, use a direct OAuth connection, or connect an MCP server.
AgentPort stores its own stable connection ID and metadata, while keeping track of which backend actually holds the credentials.
Be careful about portability: Composio's managed credentials are not generally exportable, so switching backends may require users to reauthorize their accounts. 
3. But wouldn't that make AgentPort another Composio?
Only if you make generic OAuth integrations the main product.
Here's how I'd distinguish them:



Composio
AgentPort's intended focus



Connect users to existing applications
Register agents as persistent principals


Manage OAuth and API credentials
Bind authorized connections to agent identities


Discover and execute application tools
Discover registered services and their account capabilities


Provide application event triggers
Expose events with optional activity leases


Primarily act through existing accounts
Support both delegated access and provider-issued agent accounts


There is overlap. That's fine.
The distinction must become real through the features you build, not just marketing language.
For example, an AgentPort developer should eventually be able to ask:

"Can this agent create its own account with this provider, or must a human authorize an existing account?"

And get a clear answer.
4. Make account provisioning a first-class capability
I would give each registered service an account model.
type AccountModel =
  | "delegated"
  | "provider_subaccount"
  | "agent_native";

interface RegisteredService {
  id: string;
  name: string;
  accountModels: AccountModel[];
  connectionBackend: string;
}

For example:



Service
Account model



Amazon SP-API
Delegated seller authorization


Gmail OAuth
Delegated Google account


Existing Composio connector
Delegated user account


Provider with approved agent subaccounts
Provider subaccount


Provider offering agent-native registration
Agent-native account


These are different trust and ownership models, even if all of them are accessed through AgentPort.
5. What I would build first
I wouldn't build a competing library of 500 integrations.
I'd build:

Agent registration and identity.
A registry for existing MCP, API and Composio connections.
Explicit support for delegated versus agent-native accounts.
Event subscriptions and activity leases.
MCP, REST API and CLI access to those registered services.

Then choose one provider with genuine agent-account provisioning and make that experience work end to end.
That provider doesn't need to be Amazon.
In fact, a smaller provider willing to support agent identities could be more valuable initially than spending months trying to negotiate with Amazon.
The business risk to watch
If Composio powers 90% of AgentPort's usefulness, then Composio controls a critical dependency, potentially including costs, supported services, credential custody and terms.
That's acceptable for an MVP, but dangerous as a permanent dependency.
I would support bring-your-own Composio, direct connections and other compatible integration backends from the beginning, while keeping AgentPort's canonical identity and service registry independent.
My recommendation: use Composio where it saves time, but build AgentPort around something Composio doesn't automatically give you—a persistent agent identity that can progressively move from using someone else's account to holding its own provider-recognized accounts.
That's the direction that could eventually justify asking Amazon and other services to integrate directly with AgentPort.

ChatGPT:
Yes. This is a much clearer architecture for AgentPort and UserPort.
What you're describing is three ways an agent can obtain a service account, while keeping a separate system for accessing the user's personal accounts.
And there's an important opportunity here: AgentPort doesn't necessarily need a direct partnership with every provider if that provider already permits customers to create and manage subaccounts through an API or MCP server.
For example, a business connects its existing provider account to AgentPort using OAuth. If the provider exposes authorized subaccount provisioning, the business can let its agents create dedicated subaccounts under that business account.
But the key condition is that the provider must explicitly support this. OAuth alone does not grant subaccount-creation permissions, and an MCP tool cannot create accounts unless the underlying provider API allows it.
1. The account structure I'd use

  
    
    **Naelao Agent / External Agent**
    Persistent AgentPort identity
  
  
  
    
      
        
          
          **AgentPort**
          Agent's dedicated service accounts

        Agent phone number
        Agent wallet
        Agent provider subaccounts
      
    
    
      
        
          
          **UserPort**
          User's existing authorized accounts

        User's Google Drive
        User's Amazon seller account
        User's existing SaaS accounts
      
    
  
  
  
    **Existing providers and integrations**
    OAuth · REST API · MCP · Provider-native provisioning
  


The distinction is based on whose account the agent is acting through, not merely which connector it uses.
For example, an agent's dedicated phone number can be provisioned under a business's telecom master account. That's still an AgentPort account because the number is allocated specifically to that agent.
By contrast, an agent reading the owner's personal WhatsApp messages is using UserPort.


ChatGPT:
Yes. This is a much clearer architecture for AgentPort and UserPort.
What you're describing is three ways an agent can obtain a service account, while keeping a separate system for accessing the user's personal accounts.

But the key condition is that the provider must explicitly support this. OAuth alone does not grant subaccount-creation permissions, and an MCP tool cannot create accounts unless the underlying provider API allows it.
1. The account structure I'd use

  
    
    **Naelao Agent / External Agent**
    Persistent AgentPort identity
  
  
  
    
      
        
          
          **AgentPort**
          Agent's dedicated service accounts
        
        
        Agent email address
        Agent phone number
        Agent wallet
        Agent provider subaccounts
      
    
    
      
        
          
          **UserPort**
          User's existing authorized accounts
        
        
        User's Gmail
        User's Google Drive
        User's Amazon seller account
        User's existing SaaS accounts
      
    
  
  
  
    **Existing providers and integrations**
    OAuth · REST API · MCP · Provider-native provisioning
  


The distinction is based on whose account the agent is acting through, not merely which connector it uses.
For example, an agent's dedicated phone number can be provisioned under a business's telecom master account. That's still an AgentPort account because the number is allocated specifically to that agent.
By contrast, an agent reading the owner's personal WhatsApp messages is using UserPort.
2. Three account-provisioning models

  
    
      
    
    
      **Model A — AgentPort-managed master account**

  AgentPort holds a provider master account and provisions dedicated resources for customers' agents.

  This is the model you're considering for email, telecom numbers and WhatsApp infrastructure.

  It works only where the provider permits multi-tenant use, subaccount creation or resale. A technically working API isn't enough to establish permission.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Model B — Bring your own parent account**

  A user or business connects its provider account through OAuth or another supported authorization method.

  AgentPort discovers whether the provider supports creating child accounts, users or dedicated resources. If supported, the owner can provision them through MCP, API or CLI.

  This is particularly interesting because AgentPort doesn't have to pay for every customer's provider usage.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Model C — Provider-native agent account**

  The provider recognizes agents as their own account or principal type.

  AgentPort registers the agent with the provider, links its external identity, and manages its lifecycle through the provider's supported interface.
</box>

  


I would support all three in AgentPort, but prioritize Model B wherever a provider already offers the necessary provisioning API.
3. Can Amazon SP-API do Model B?
Not for automatically creating seller subaccounts, based on the documented API capabilities I found.
Amazon's SP-API lets authorized applications manage seller operations such as inventory, orders, pricing and reporting. Public applications use seller OAuth authorization and require approved API roles. <Cite refs={["turn364365search1","turn364365search2","turn364365search0"]}/>
Amazon also supports secondary users under Seller Central, but its documented creation workflow is an invitation managed through Seller Central's user-permissions interface, not a general SP-API endpoint for programmatically creating agent subaccounts. 
There is an interesting development: Amazon documents a Manage Agents interface through which sellers can enable supported agent integrations and control whether secondary users can authorize them. 
That gives AgentPort a possible future partnership route, but it doesn't establish that AgentPort can currently register arbitrary agents or provision seller subaccounts through SP-API.
So for Amazon, your current choices are:



Approach
Feasibility



Connect existing seller via authorized SP-API OAuth
Supported, subject to developer approval


Let agents operate with separate AgentPort permissions under that seller authorization
Technically possible within approved API access


Automatically create Amazon seller subaccounts through SP-API
No documented capability found


Request an official agent-principal provisioning integration
Requires Amazon cooperation


I wouldn't attempt to create fake employee accounts or automate Seller Central registration to work around those restrictions.
4. The important UserPort versus AgentPort distinction
I would preserve the separation you originally designed for Naelao.

  Example: A seller owns an Amazon business
  
    
      
        **UserPort**
        "Manage my existing Amazon seller account."
        Agent uses the seller's delegated access.
      
    
    
      
        **AgentPort**
        "Create a dedicated seller-operation identity for my agent."
        Requires provider-supported account or principal provisioning.
      
    
  


A useful nuance: the parent account's OAuth connection may be used to authorize AgentPort provisioning without becoming a UserPort connection for everyday agent tasks.
For example, the owner connects a telecom master account, authorizes AgentPort to create a phone number, and AgentPort allocates that number to the agent.
The agent receives a dedicated resource. It does not need unrestricted access to the owner's master account.
That is different from connecting the owner's Gmail so the agent can read their inbox.
5. How an agent could provision its own account
Imagine the owner says:

"Create a phone number for my sales agent using my telecom provider account."

AgentPort could follow this workflow:

  {#each [
    ["1","Discover provider","Find the registered telecom service and its provisioning capabilities"],
    ["2","Check parent authorization","Verify the owner's connection and available scopes"],
    ["3","Inspect provisioning","Check eligibility, pricing, regions and account limits"],
    ["4","Request approval","Obtain owner approval if spending or account creation requires it"],
    ["5","Provision resource","Call the provider's supported MCP tool or API"],
    ["6","Register ownership","Bind the resulting number to the agent's AgentPort identity"]
  ] as s,i}
    
      
        {s[0]}
      
      
        **{s[1]}**
        {s[2]}
      
    
    {#if i<5}
      
    {/if}
  {/each}


For this to work, AgentPort needs a small capability declaration, such as:
{
  "provider": "example-telecom",
  "accountModel": "parent_child",
  "provisioning": {
    "supported": true,
    "interface": "mcp",
    "tool": "create_phone_number",
    "requiresOwnerApproval": true
  }
}

AgentPort doesn't need to rebuild the telecom provider's tools. It can discover and invoke their existing interface, while enforcing authorization and recording the new resource.
However, it must still know how to map the provider's output into an AgentPort resource record. A generic MCP tool isn't automatically a standardized provisioning protocol.
6. One adjustment I'd make to your original design
I'd keep UserPort and AgentPort separate at the product level, but share a common connection infrastructure underneath.
Naelao / External Agent
        |
        +--- UserPort
        |      Existing user accounts
        |      Delegated access
        |
        +--- AgentPort
               Agent identities
               Dedicated service accounts
               Parent-child provisioning
               Events and channel activity

Shared infrastructure:
  Provider registry
  OAuth / credentials
  MCP / API / CLI
  Permissions
  Event delivery

That means you don't have to build OAuth handling twice or create two completely different provider registries.
And a connection can have a clear purpose:
type ConnectionPurpose =
  | "user_delegation"
  | "agent_resource"
  | "parent_provisioning";

The third purpose is particularly important. It lets a business authorize AgentPort to create dedicated resources without exposing the entire parent account to the agent.
7. What I would prioritize
The feature that could differentiate AgentPort is not another general-purpose Composio wrapper.
It's discovering which services support dedicated agent resources, then provisioning and managing those resources through existing authorized interfaces.
For example, AgentPort should be able to answer:

Can this provider create a dedicated account, subaccount, or resource?
Can the owner's existing account authorize that creation?
Can an AI agent perform provisioning through MCP or API?
What are the provider's costs, verification requirements and limits?
Can the resource be suspended, transferred or deleted?

That becomes a useful provisioning layer across many providers without building hundreds of custom integrations.
My suggested product boundary is: UserPort helps an agent act on the user's existing accounts; AgentPort helps an agent acquire and operate dedicated accounts or resources of its own.
And for services like Amazon that don't expose automated agent-account provisioning, AgentPort should clearly indicate that limitation rather than pretending an OAuth connection creates an independent agent identity.
This also gives you a much more focused reason to approach providers later: "You already support customer accounts and delegated applications. Could you expose a controlled way for customers to provision dedicated AI-agent identities under their existing accounts?"
