Plus if I target smaller services they become more profitable with hundreds of agents prefering agent port because essentially amazon or other services will ban them then after a while the big services might start to see that if they don't adopt they might loose


ChatGPT:
Yes. That's a plausible bottom-up market-entry strategy for AgentPort: start with smaller services that welcome autonomous agents, help them attract usage and revenue, then use that demand to persuade larger platforms to support agent-native accounts.
The opportunity is that AgentPort could become a distribution channel for agent-friendly services, rather than just infrastructure for connecting agents.
How the strategy could work

  
    
    **Thousands of agents using AgentPort**
    Need accounts, payments, communications and services
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Service Discovery**
    Find providers that explicitly support agent accounts and provisioning
  
  
  
    {#each ["Small SaaS","Agent-friendly commerce","Infrastructure providers"] as x}
      
        
          
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    **More provider revenue and agent adoption**
    Evidence for attracting additional providers
  


Imagine an agent needs an email address, phone number, payment account, or cloud service. If several providers are available, AgentPort can surface those that support automated provisioning, clear pricing, and agent-friendly policies.
That creates an incentive for smaller providers to integrate.
The network effect you're aiming for

  
    
    
      **More agents join**

  Developers choose AgentPort because they can provision useful accounts without repeatedly navigating manual registration.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Smaller providers gain revenue**

  AgentPort routes legitimate demand to providers that support agent-operated accounts.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Larger providers notice**

  If meaningful customer demand shifts toward agent-friendly alternatives, larger platforms may have an incentive to introduce comparable capabilities.
</box>

  


But there's a critical distinction: hundreds of agents do not necessarily mean hundreds of profitable customers.
A provider might gain 1,000 agent accounts but lose money if those agents generate excessive API traffic, fraud risk, support costs, or very little paid usage.
You need to demonstrate profitable agent activity, not merely account registrations.
An illustrative economic example
{@body const [agents,setAgents] = DIL.useState(1000)}{@body const [spend,setSpend] = DIL.useState(12)}{@body const [margin,setMargin] = DIL.useState(40)}{@body const [share,setShare] = DIL.useState(10)}{@body const gross = agentsspend}  Provider economics simulator            Active paying agents      {agents.toLocaleString("en-US")}                      Monthly spend per agent      ${spend}                      Provider gross margin before referral fees      {margin}%                      AgentPort referral share of revenue      {share}%                                Provider monthly revenue        ${gross.toLocaleString("en-US")}                            AgentPort monthly referral revenue        ${(gross</em>share/100).toLocaleString("en-US",{maximumFractionDigits:0})}                            Provider contribution after referral        ${(gross*(margin-share)/100).toLocaleString("en-US",{maximumFractionDigits:0})}                            Annual provider revenue        ${(gross*12).toLocaleString("en-US")}              Hypothetical economics only. Contribution excludes additional acquisition, compliance, integration, fraud and support costs.
One thing I would change in your competitive thesis
I wouldn't build the strategy around Amazon or other large services banning agents.
Large platforms may eventually offer their own agent products, approve selected integrations, or restrict third-party access. They may also have network advantages that smaller providers cannot easily replicate.
Instead, build around a positive proposition:
Agent-friendly providers gain access to customers and transactions they otherwise wouldn't reach.
And AgentPort helps agents choose services based on capabilities, price, reliability, eligibility and supported account models.
For example, an agent looking for cloud storage might be able to choose an agent-friendly provider without caring whether it's the largest brand.
But for something like Amazon's consumer marketplace, the network of buyers, sellers, products and logistics matters enormously. A small alternative isn't automatically interchangeable.
How to turn this into negotiating power
I would measure four things from the beginning: active paying agents, actual transaction volume, provider revenue attributable to AgentPort, and how many agents specifically request unavailable providers.
Then, when approaching a large service, you can demonstrate that developers already want to use it through AgentPort, while also showing that other providers are generating legitimate business from agent-native access.
The strongest long-term position is not "integrate with us or lose." It's "we can bring you a growing, measurable market of verified AI-agent customers through one integration."
If you can prove that, AgentPort becomes much more interesting to providers of every size.


You:
What about provider that supports agent port buy requires payment to for some services 
Like Whatsapp has limits and have to pay but dies it support payment via agentport s supported payment providers


ChatGPT:
Yes. This introduces another important part of AgentPort: how agents pay for the services they provision and consume.
Imagine an agent creates its own WhatsApp Business integration, phone number, cloud account, or API subscription through AgentPort.
The service may require payment before provisioning, charge per message, or bill monthly.
Your idea would let the agent use a payment method supported by AgentPort to pay the provider, rather than forcing every agent developer to manually configure billing with every service.
But there's a catch: a provider supporting AgentPort identity or provisioning doesn't automatically mean it accepts payments through AgentPort. Payment support needs to be negotiated or implemented through an existing payment interface.
I would treat payment as a separate capability of each registered provider.


ChatGPT:
Yes. This introduces another important part of AgentPort: how agents pay for the services they provision and consume.
Imagine an agent creates its own WhatsApp Business integration, phone number, cloud account, or API subscription through AgentPort.
The service may require payment before provisioning, charge per message, or bill monthly.
Your idea would let the agent use a payment method supported by AgentPort to pay the provider, rather than forcing every agent developer to manually configure billing with every service.
But there's a catch: a provider supporting AgentPort identity or provisioning doesn't automatically mean it accepts payments through AgentPort. Payment support needs to be negotiated or implemented through an existing payment interface.
I would treat payment as a separate capability of each registered provider.
How WhatsApp actually handles payment
According to Meta's current WhatsApp Business documentation, WhatsApp Business Platform charges depend on message category and destination. From October 2026, the first 1,000 service messages per business phone number each month are free, with subsequent service messages charged. Other message categories have their own rates. 
Meta supports several billing arrangements:



Billing method
Can AgentPort use it?



Business's own credit/debit card
Yes, if the business configures it with Meta


Meta-approved credit line
Only for eligible businesses


WhatsApp Solution Partner billing
Potentially, through an approved partner arrangement


Arbitrary AgentPort wallet
Not natively documented as a Meta payment method


AgentPort-supported virtual card
Potentially, if Meta accepts the card and the payment provider permits this use


Meta's partner documentation says Solution Partners can manage billing through shared credit lines, while ordinary Tech Partners cannot offer the same billing arrangement. 
So an agent cannot simply say, "Pay Meta using my AgentPort wallet," unless there's an authorized payment route connecting that wallet to Meta's accepted billing methods.
What AgentPort should support
I'd add a Payment Capability Registry to your existing service registry.
Each service declares how it accepts payment.
type BillingModel =
  | "provider_direct"
  | "agentport_managed"
  | "partner_billed"
  | "invoice"
  | "prepaid";

interface ServiceBilling {
  model: BillingModel;
  supportedPaymentMethods: string[];
  estimatedCosts?: boolean;
  usageMetering?: boolean;
  requiresOwnerApproval?: boolean;
}

Then AgentPort can route payments through three models.

  
    
    **Agent needs paid service**
    Provision resource or continue usage
  
  
  
    
      
        
        Direct billing
        Provider charges owner's approved payment method
      
    
    
      
        
        AgentPort billing
        AgentPort collects funds and pays provider where permitted
      
    
    
      
        
        Partner billing
        Authorized partner bills for provider usage
      
    
  


Example: An agent wants to send 10,000 WhatsApp messages
AgentPort should:

Check whether the agent has an authorized WhatsApp Business connection.
Determine which messages are billable and estimate the cost using the current rate card.
Check the agent's spending limit and owner's approval policy.
Verify that the connected WhatsApp account has a valid billing arrangement.
Execute messages within provider messaging limits, recording actual usage and charges.

Crucially, an AgentPort spending limit doesn't automatically cap the provider's bill. For postpaid services, you'd need usage monitoring, reservation of estimated costs where feasible, and suspension controls. Delayed usage reporting can still produce overages.
The more interesting long-term opportunity
Imagine a small cloud provider integrates with AgentPort and declares:
{
  "service": "example-cloud",
  "agentAccounts": true,
  "billing": {
    "model": "prepaid",
    "methods": ["card", "supported_wallet"],
    "minimumBalance": 5
  }
}

Now an agent could request a $5 cloud account, obtain authorization, pay through a supported payment method, and start operating it without manually visiting the provider's website.
That could become a significant advantage for smaller agent-friendly providers.
But I would not build AgentPort into a bank or universal payment processor initially. Let licensed payment providers handle money movement and payment instruments. AgentPort manages payment authorization, budgets, provider billing metadata and transaction records.
The important distinction is:
AgentPort identity determines who the agent is. AgentPort provisioning determines which services it can obtain. AgentPort payment authorization determines who is allowed to spend money and how the provider gets paid.
Those are three separate capabilities that work together.
And this gives you a potentially powerful feature: an agent could discover a service, create its own authorized account, obtain funding approval, pay for it, and start using it—all through AgentPort's MCP, API or CLI interface.

How WhatsApp actually handles payment
According to Meta's current WhatsApp Business documentation, WhatsApp Business Platform charges depend on message category and destination. From October 2026, the first 1,000 service messages per business phone number each month are free, with subsequent service messages charged. Other message categories have their own rates. 
Meta supports several billing arrangements:



Billing method
Can AgentPort use it?



Business's own credit/debit card
Yes, if the business configures it with Meta


Meta-approved credit line
Only for eligible businesses


WhatsApp Solution Partner billing
Potentially, through an approved partner arrangement


Arbitrary AgentPort wallet
Not natively documented as a Meta payment method


AgentPort-supported virtual card
Potentially, if Meta accepts the card and the payment provider permits this use


Meta's partner documentation says Solution Partners can manage billing through shared credit lines, while ordinary Tech Partners cannot offer the same billing arrangement. 
So an agent cannot simply say, "Pay Meta using my AgentPort wallet," unless there's an authorized payment route connecting that wallet to Meta's accepted billing methods.
What AgentPort should support
I'd add a Payment Capability Registry to your existing service registry.
Each service declares how it accepts payment.
type BillingModel =
  | "provider_direct"
  | "agentport_managed"
  | "partner_billed"
  | "invoice"
  | "prepaid";

interface ServiceBilling {
  model: BillingModel;
  supportedPaymentMethods: string[];
  estimatedCosts?: boolean;
  usageMetering?: boolean;
  requiresOwnerApproval?: boolean;
}

Then AgentPort can route payments through three models.

  
    
    **Agent needs paid service**
    Provision resource or continue usage
  
  
  
    
      
        
        Direct billing
        Provider charges owner's approved payment method
      
    
    
      
        
        AgentPort billing
        AgentPort collects funds and pays provider where permitted
      
    
    
      
        
        Partner billing
        Authorized partner bills for provider usage
      
    
  


Example: An agent wants to send 10,000 WhatsApp messages
AgentPort should:

Check whether the agent has an authorized WhatsApp Business connection.
Determine which messages are billable and estimate the cost using the current rate card.
Check the agent's spending limit and owner's approval policy.
Verify that the connected WhatsApp account has a valid billing arrangement.
Execute messages within provider messaging limits, recording actual usage and charges.

Crucially, an AgentPort spending limit doesn't automatically cap the provider's bill. For postpaid services, you'd need usage monitoring, reservation of estimated costs where feasible, and suspension controls. Delayed usage reporting can still produce overages.
The more interesting long-term opportunity
Imagine a small cloud provider integrates with AgentPort and declares:
{
  "service": "example-cloud",
  "agentAccounts": true,
  "billing": {
    "model": "prepaid",
    "methods": ["card", "supported_wallet"],
    "minimumBalance": 5
  }
}

Now an agent could request a $5 cloud account, obtain authorization, pay through a supported payment method, and start operating it without manually visiting the provider's website.
That could become a significant advantage for smaller agent-friendly providers.
But I would not build AgentPort into a bank or universal payment processor initially. Let licensed payment providers handle money movement and payment instruments. AgentPort manages payment authorization, budgets, provider billing metadata and transaction records.
The important distinction is:
AgentPort identity determines who the agent is. AgentPort provisioning determines which services it can obtain. AgentPort payment authorization determines who is allowed to spend money and how the provider gets paid.
Those are three separate capabilities that work together.
And this gives you a potentially powerful feature: an agent could discover a service, create its own authorized account, obtain funding approval, pay for it, and start using it—all through AgentPort's MCP, API or CLI interface.

I checked Stripe's latest agent offerings, and this is very relevant to AgentPort. Stripe now supports several forms of agent identity and agent payments, including one that closely matches your parent-account idea.
And I agree with your correction: payments should just be another provider registered in AgentPort, not a separate payment system that AgentPort builds itself.
1. What Stripe actually supports

  
    
    
      **Stripe agent-tagged API keys**

  Stripe published guidance on October 21, 2026 explaining how to give an AI agent its own long-lived operational identity within an existing Stripe account. The agent can have permissions and approval rules independent of an individual employee's login.

  <badge color="success">Parent-account agent identity</badge>

  <text size="xs" color="secondary">Not an independent merchant account. <Cite ref="turn522659search8"/></text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Link wallet for agents**

  Launched April 29, 2026. Agents can request one-time-use cards or Shared Payment Tokens backed by a human's existing payment methods. Currently, each spend request requires the person's approval; Stripe plans broader spending controls.

  <badge>Delegated user payment</badge>

  <text size="xs" color="secondary"><Cite ref="turn522659search2"/></text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Stripe Issuing for agents**

  Lets eligible businesses or platforms issue agent-specific virtual cards with spending controls and transaction visibility.

  <badge>Dedicated agent payment instrument</badge>

  <text size="xs" color="secondary">Private preview; eligibility and onboarding apply. <Cite ref="turn522659search6"/></text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Stripe Connect**

  Platforms can onboard connected merchant accounts, but those accounts represent real eligible businesses or individuals with contractual and verification requirements.

  <badge>Platform-connected merchant accounts</badge>

  <text size="xs" color="secondary">Not automatically agent-owned accounts. <Cite ref="turn522659search3"/></text>
</box>

  


The particularly interesting development is that Stripe now distinguishes between an agent acting through a user's OAuth authorization and an agent operating under its own agent-tagged API credential.
That is very close to the distinction you've been making between UserPort and AgentPort.


ChatGPT:

And I agree with your correction: payments should just be another provider registered in AgentPort, not a separate payment system that AgentPort builds itself.
1. What Stripe actually supports

</box>

  Platforms can onboard connected merchant accounts, but those accounts represent real eligible businesses or individuals with contractual and verification requirements.

  <badge>Platform-connected merchant accounts</badge>

  <text size="xs" color="secondary">Not automatically agent-owned accounts. <Cite ref="turn522659search3"/></text>
</box>

  


The particularly interesting development is that Stripe now distinguishes between an agent acting through a user's OAuth authorization and an agent operating under its own agent-tagged API credential.
That is very close to the distinction you've been making between UserPort and AgentPort.
2. Which Stripe model fits AgentPort?



Stripe capability
Your architecture



OAuth access to owner's Stripe account
UserPort


Agent-tagged API key under owner's Stripe account
AgentPort — parent-owned agent identity


Agent-specific virtual card through Issuing
AgentPort — dedicated payment resource


Stripe Connect account
Provider-connected business account, subject to verification


Link wallet payment on behalf of owner
UserPort payment authorization


An agent-tagged key is especially interesting because it gives an agent independent operational credentials without requiring a new Stripe merchant account.
However, I couldn't verify that Stripe currently provides a public API for automatically creating those agent-tagged keys. Its October guidance describes creating them in the Dashboard. So AgentPort should not assume fully automated provisioning yet. 
3. Could AgentPort own the parent Stripe account?
Potentially, through Stripe Issuing for agents, but not as an unrestricted shared merchant account.
Stripe specifically describes a platform model in which a company can issue cards for agents operating on behalf of its customers. That sounds close to your AgentPort-managed master-account model. However, it's a private-preview product, and Stripe would need to approve the platform and its use case. 

  
    
    **AgentPort Platform**
    Approved Stripe Issuing integration
  
  
  
    **Stripe**
    Card issuing, payment controls and transaction processing
  
  
  
    {#each ["Agent A card","Agent B card","Agent C card"] as name}
      
        
          
          {name}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  Conceptual structure. Actual cardholders, funding arrangements and permitted customer relationships depend on Stripe approval.


That's much more realistic than opening ordinary Stripe merchant accounts for thousands of AI agents under AgentPort's name.
4. The biggest discovery: Stripe is also entering service provisioning
Stripe now describes Stripe Projects, where software providers can let agents discover services, create accounts on behalf of humans, retrieve credentials and manage billing programmatically.
It also supports machine-payment flows for services using protocols such as MPP and x402. 
This is particularly important because Stripe is expanding beyond simply processing payments into parts of the agent-service onboarding workflow.
That creates both an opportunity and a competitive consideration for AgentPort.
AgentPort could register Stripe Projects as an existing provisioning service rather than building competing connectors. But Stripe may also become a competitor in service discovery and account provisioning.
5. Can Stripe help agents pay almost any provider?
Yes, potentially, but there are two distinct mechanisms.
Virtual cards: An eligible agent can receive a card through Stripe Issuing and use it at merchants that accept that card, subject to card-network rules, merchant acceptance and authorization controls.
Shared Payment Tokens: An agent can use a scoped payment credential with compatible sellers. Stripe has also expanded support for Visa and Mastercard agentic network tokens. 
For a WhatsApp-related service, that means a Stripe-backed card might pay the service provider if it accepts the card. It doesn't automatically make Meta accept AgentPort billing or allow AgentPort to bypass Meta's account requirements.
6. How I would register Stripe inside AgentPort
{
  "provider": "stripe",
  "capabilities": {
    "delegatedAccountAccess": true,
    "agentTaggedCredentials": true,
    "agentVirtualCards": "restricted_preview",
    "agentPaymentTokens": true,
    "connectedMerchantAccounts": true
  },
  "interfaces": [
    "api",
    "mcp"
  ]
}

The registry would describe capabilities, not promise that every capability is available to every developer. Stripe's MCP server also doesn't necessarily expose every Issuing or account-management operation.
My recommendation: Keep Stripe as an external payment provider, just like telecom, email and cloud providers. Support user-connected Stripe accounts first, then provider-issued agent credentials, and eventually an approved AgentPort-managed Issuing arrangement if there's enough demand.
One particularly interesting question for your strategy is whether Stripe Projects could serve as an initial source of agent-friendly providers for AgentPort, while AgentPort concentrates on persistent agent identity and dedicated account ownership. That would let you benefit from Stripe's growing ecosystem without depending on it for everything.

W ha t about Coinbase search web


ChatGPT:
Coinbase is actually one of the strongest examples I've found for your AgentPort idea. It already supports both dedicated agent wallets and parent-owned agent subaccounts, and it has built payment infrastructure specifically for AI agents.
Even better, Coinbase already supports MCP and CLI access, so AgentPort wouldn't need to invent its own Coinbase connector.
1. What Coinbase offers for agents

  
    
    
      ****

  Launched in June 2026. Users can connect existing Coinbase accounts to AI agents, with isolated agent portfolios, trading controls and spending limits.

  **Type:** Parent account with isolated agent portfolio.

  <text color="secondary" size="xs">Coinbase says agents cannot withdraw funds off Coinbase through this product. <Cite refs={["turn339011search0","turn339011search3"]}/></text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      ****

  Announced February 2026. Agents receive dedicated non-custodial crypto wallets, with programmable security controls and x402 payment capabilities.

  **Type:** Dedicated agent wallet, provisioned through Coinbase Developer Platform.

  <text color="secondary" size="xs"><Cite ref="turn339011search1"/></text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      ** and x402**

  Lets agents access wallet functionality and pay compatible online services using stablecoins, including paid APIs and data.

  **Type:** Payment-provider integration, not a separate AgentPort payment system.

  <text color="secondary" size="xs"><Cite ref="turn339011search4"/></text>
</box>

  


2. Can AgentPort manage the parent account?
For Coinbase Developer Platform wallets, yes, technically. Coinbase documents server-controlled wallets created programmatically through its API.
For example, AgentPort could operate an approved CDP project and create distinct wallets for agents:
const wallet = await cdp.evm.createAccount();

console.log(wallet.address);

Coinbase also supports named accounts and smart accounts with programmable spending policies. 
That resembles your original master-account approach for phone numbers and email addresses.
But there is an important distinction:

A dedicated crypto wallet is not a dedicated Coinbase exchange account.
A CDP project can provision wallets, but it doesn't automatically grant AgentPort permission to operate a custodial financial service for customers.
Custodial accounts, trading and certain payment APIs require business verification and additional approval. 

So AgentPort-managed wallet provisioning looks technically feasible, subject to Coinbase's commercial terms, supported regions and custody model. AgentPort-managed exchange subaccounts would be a separate proposition.

2. Can AgentPort manage the parent account?
For Coinbase Developer Platform wallets, yes, technically. Coinbase documents server-controlled wallets created programmatically through its API.
For example, AgentPort could operate an approved CDP project and create distinct wallets for agents:
const wallet = await cdp.evm.createAccount();

console.log(wallet.address);

Coinbase also supports named accounts and smart accounts with programmable spending policies. 
That resembles your original master-account approach for phone numbers and email addresses.
But there is an important distinction:

A dedicated crypto wallet is not a dedicated Coinbase exchange account.
A CDP project can provision wallets, but it doesn't automatically grant AgentPort permission to operate a custodial financial service for customers.
Custodial accounts, trading and certain payment APIs require business verification and additional approval. 

So AgentPort-managed wallet provisioning looks technically feasible, subject to Coinbase's commercial terms, supported regions and custody model. AgentPort-managed exchange subaccounts would be a separate proposition.
3. How Coinbase fits your AgentPort and UserPort model



Coinbase product
AgentPort or UserPort?
Account structure



Coinbase for Agents
UserPort initially
User's Coinbase account with isolated agent portfolio


CDP Agentic Wallets
AgentPort
Dedicated wallet for each agent


CDP server wallets
AgentPort
Wallet provisioned under a developer's CDP project


Coinbase Payments MCP
Either
Access to wallet and payment capabilities


x402 payments
AgentPort capability
Agent pays compatible services using an authorized wallet


The important point is that Coinbase's agent portfolio isn't necessarily an independent legal Coinbase customer account. It remains tied to the user's account.
A dedicated onchain wallet, however, can have its own address and signing authority without needing an independent Coinbase exchange account.
4. What this means for your architecture

  
    
    **Naelao or External Agent**
  
  
  
    
      
        **AgentPort**
        Dedicated Coinbase wallet
        Own address, permissions and spending policies
      
    
    
      
        **UserPort**
        User-authorized Coinbase access
        Existing Coinbase account and isolated trading portfolio
      
    
  
  
  
    **Coinbase**
    Wallet APIs · MCP · CLI · x402
  


I would start with bring-your-own Coinbase Developer Platform project, so developers can provision wallets without AgentPort assuming custody or funding responsibility.
Later, an AgentPort-managed CDP project could offer one-click wallet provisioning, provided Coinbase's terms and applicable financial regulations allow the intended multi-tenant model.
5. Why Coinbase is strategically important
Coinbase's x402 protocol is particularly interesting because it allows compatible services to charge agents directly for API calls, data and other resources.
An AgentPort agent could discover a paid service, inspect the price, receive spending authorization, and pay through its Coinbase wallet.
That gives you a payment option without AgentPort building a payment processor.
However, x402 does not make every website or SaaS subscription payable with Coinbase. The merchant must support the relevant payment mechanism.
Also, Coinbase's own AgentKit documentation warns that simply giving an LLM access to a funded wallet can expose it to prompt-injection risks. Spending limits, recipient allowlists and approval checks should be enforced outside the model. 
My assessment
Coinbase is a particularly promising early provider for AgentPort because it already has:

Programmatic wallet provisioning.
Dedicated agent financial resources.
Existing MCP and CLI interfaces.
Machine-to-machine payment support.
Provider-enforced controls in some of its agent products.

You can explore the official offerings at  and .
One strategic consideration: Coinbase is already building substantial agent infrastructure itself. So AgentPort's advantage should be that an agent can use Coinbase alongside Stripe and other providers through one persistent identity, rather than competing with Coinbase to become the wallet provider.
The combination of Coinbase for crypto-native payments and Stripe for card-based payments could give AgentPort broad payment coverage, while both remain independent providers.

You:
So agentport should be able to support all type from agent accounts to parent subaccounts to agent port as parent


ChatGPT:
Exactly. AgentPort should be account-model agnostic. It should support whichever account structure a provider permits, rather than forcing every service into one model.
I'd define four account models:

  
    
      
      **1. Agent-native account**
    
    The provider recognizes the agent as its own account or principal.
    
      Provider → Agent Account
    
  
  
  
    
      
      **2. User-owned parent account**
    
    The owner connects a provider account and authorizes a dedicated agent subaccount, credential or resource.
    
      User Account → Agent Subaccount
    
  
  
  
    
      
      **3. AgentPort-managed parent account**
    
    AgentPort provisions dedicated agent resources under its provider account, where multi-tenant provisioning is permitted.
    
      AgentPort Account → Agent Resource
    
  
  
  
    
      
      **4. Delegated account access**
    
    The agent acts on an existing user account without receiving its own account or dedicated resource.
    
      User Account → OAuth → Agent
    
    Primarily UserPort, but supported by shared connection infrastructure.
  


One unified account record
You don't need four different provisioning systems. You need a common record that describes the account relationship.
type AccountModel =
  | "agent_native"
  | "user_parent"
  | "agentport_parent"
  | "delegated";

interface AgentServiceAccount {
  id: string;
  agentId: string;
  providerId: string;

  model: AccountModel;

  parentAccountId?: string;
  externalAccountId?: string;

  credentialRef?: string;
  provisioningStatus:
    | "pending"
    | "active"
    | "suspended"
    | "revoked";
}

The actual creation action comes from the provider's existing MCP server, API or other supported interface. AgentPort handles registration, authorization, lifecycle records and access.
Example provider capabilities



Provider capability
AgentPort behavior



Creates native agent accounts
Register the agent directly


Creates subaccounts under user
Connect parent, provision child


Allows platform-managed resources
Provision under AgentPort parent


Only supports OAuth
Route delegated access through UserPort


Supports multiple models
Let owner choose an eligible model


Importantly, an agent-specific API key, phone number, wallet or subaccount is not necessarily a legally independent account. AgentPort should preserve the provider's actual ownership and responsibility structure.
The feature I'd make central
Imagine an agent requests:

"I need a phone number, email address, wallet and cloud account."

AgentPort discovers available providers, checks their supported account models and returns options. The owner can choose between using their own parent account, an AgentPort-managed option, or a native agent account where supported.
The key is that the agent shouldn't need to understand every provider's account structure. AgentPort abstracts the provisioning process while retaining the underlying ownership, permissions, billing and compliance rules.
And I'd keep your original product separation:

AgentPort: Dedicated agent accounts, subaccounts, identities and resources.
UserPort: Authorized access to the user's existing accounts.

Both can share the same provider registry, credential infrastructure and MCP/API/CLI interfaces.
That gives AgentPort a flexible foundation without turning it into another Composio or a payment processor.


You:
What about accounts that requires other accounts ie for humans web login user when user needs a website acc they need an email acc and phone number . And essentially an agent cauld do the same like grokbot just uses it's own accounts to create accounts of various services but most agents can't act like humans plus most sites ban this .
(A quick question for naelao like in grokbot is it possible like technically to use composio userport for user to say add their service accounts and the ai can use that account via browser automation how dies grokbot do it )


ChatGPT:
Yes. You're identifying another important category for AgentPort: accounts that depend on other accounts to be created or recovered.
For example, an AI agent might need its own email address and phone number before it can register with a website. AgentPort could provision those first, then use them during the website's approved registration process.
But there's an important distinction between having the technical ability to create an account and being authorized by the website to create one.
1. Account dependencies in AgentPort

  
    
    **Agent Identity**
  
  
  
    
      
        
        **Agent Email**
        Verification and recovery
      
    
    
      
        
        **Agent Phone**
        SMS and account verification
      
    
  
  
  
    **Third-party Website Account**
    Created through a provider-supported API or permitted browser registration
  


AgentPort could maintain a dependency relationship:
{
  "account": "example-saas-agent-123",
  "dependencies": [
    "agent-email-123",
    "agent-phone-123"
  ],
  "registrationMethod": "browser",
  "ownership": "agent_dedicated"
}

That would be useful for account recovery, ownership transfers and auditing.

ChatGPT:
Yes. You're identifying another important category for AgentPort: accounts that depend on other accounts to be created or recovered.
For example, an AI agent might need its own email address and phone number before it can register with a website. AgentPort could provision those first, then use them during the website's approved registration process.
But there's an important distinction between having the technical ability to create an account and being authorized by the website to create one.
1. Account dependencies in AgentPort

  
    
    **Agent Identity**
  
  
  
    
      
        
        **Agent Email**
        Verification and recovery
      
    
    
      
        
        **Agent Phone**
        SMS and account verification
      
    
  
  
  
    **Third-party Website Account**
    Created through a provider-supported API or permitted browser registration
  


AgentPort could maintain a dependency relationship:
{
  "account": "example-saas-agent-123",
  "dependencies": [
    "agent-email-123",
    "agent-phone-123"
  ],
  "registrationMethod": "browser",
  "ownership": "agent_dedicated"
}

That would be useful for account recovery, ownership transfers and auditing.
However, not every email or phone number is eligible for every website. Some websites reject virtual numbers, require a real human, prohibit automated signups, or require identity verification.
I wouldn't have AgentPort bypass those restrictions.
2. Can Naelao use Composio UserPort accounts for browser automation?
Yes, but not by automatically converting a Composio OAuth connection into a browser login.
This is the important technical distinction:



Authentication
What it gives Naelao



Composio OAuth connection
Authorized API calls through Composio


Browser session
Cookies and authenticated website access


Password manager or vault
Credentials for an approved browser login


AgentPort email/phone
Verification and recovery channels for dedicated agent accounts


Composio stores OAuth credentials and executes authorized API calls without exposing the tokens to your app or agent. Those tokens generally cannot be exported and used to create a browser session. 
So, for example, connecting Gmail through UserPort lets a Naelao agent read or send emails through supported API tools. It does not automatically log the agent into Gmail's website.
However, Naelao can give the same agent both Composio tools and browser automation.
3. How Grok Bot does it
I found the relevant documentation for .
It uses a combination of persistent cloud computers, connected applications, browser sessions and a credential vault.
According to its documentation:

Each user gets a persistent cloud computer with browser sessions and logins retained between tasks.
Connected apps provide structured API access where available.
A credential vault or password manager can fill login forms without showing secrets to the model.
For passkeys, CAPTCHAs, SSO or other human-only checks, the user takes over the browser.
Bots can use their own email addresses for website registration, subject to approval and website restrictions. <Cite refs={["turn214497search0","turn214497search1"]}/>

That means Grok Bot isn't necessarily obtaining special browser access through OAuth. It maintains both structured app connections and authenticated browser environments.
4. How I'd implement this in Naelao

  
    
    **Naelao Agent**
    Chooses the appropriate access method
  
  
  
    
      
        **API path**
        
          UserPort → Composio
        
        Gmail, GitHub, Slack and other supported APIs
      
    
    
      
        **Browser path**
        
          Browser Runtime → Saved Session
        
        Website interfaces, visual workflows and unsupported API actions
      
    
  
  
  
    **Website or Online Service**
    Access remains subject to the provider's permissions and policies
  


For Naelao, I'd use this sequence:

The user connects Gmail through Composio UserPort.
The agent uses Gmail's API for normal email operations.
If a task requires a website interface, Naelao launches an isolated persistent browser.
The user signs in securely to that website once.
Naelao retains the browser session securely and reuses it for authorized tasks.
If the session expires or verification is required, the user takes over again.

This is technically feasible using a browser automation runtime such as Playwright or an existing browser-control service. OpenClaw already demonstrates a similar approach using managed browser profiles and user-attached Chrome sessions. <Cite refs={["turn214497search2","turn214497search3"]}/>
One critical requirement: isolate browser profiles by user and appropriate security boundary. Don't share one browser profile containing unrelated customers' logins.
5. What about agents creating their own website accounts?
I would make AgentPort support an additional provisioning method:
browser_assisted
The agent can use its dedicated AgentPort email, phone number and approved browser runtime to register with a website where automated registration is permitted.
The process could pause for human approval or verification where required.
Once the account exists, AgentPort registers it as a dedicated agent account and maintains references to its credentials, recovery channels and authenticated browser profile.
But this must not be treated as a way around websites that prohibit bots or require human-owned accounts.
6. Keep the product responsibilities clear
AgentPort manages dedicated agent accounts, account dependencies, provisioning methods and ownership.
UserPort manages the user's delegated accounts, including Composio connections and references to authorized browser sessions.
Naelao supplies the actual browser runtime, computer-use capabilities, workflow execution and user takeover interface.
This is important because AgentPort should remain useful to external frameworks without requiring them to use Naelao's browser automation.
The interesting long-term opportunity
Eventually, AgentPort could register a dedicated agent account regardless of how it was provisioned:

Native provider API
Parent-owned subaccount
AgentPort-managed parent account
Permitted browser-assisted registration

All four could produce the same AgentPort account record, with different ownership, authorization and credential arrangements.
So yes: Naelao can combine Composio UserPort connections with Grok Bot-style browser automation. But Composio OAuth and browser authentication are two different systems. I'd implement them as complementary capabilities rather than trying to force one to behave like the other.

You:
Can there be a scenario of circular dependencies on agentport


ChatGPT:
Yes. Circular dependencies could become a serious problem in AgentPort, especially when agents autonomously create accounts, provision resources, configure payment methods, and manage recovery credentials.
And there are actually two different problems: circular provisioning dependencies and circular operational dependencies.
1. Example: A circular account dependency
Imagine an agent wants to create a cloud account.

  
    {#each [{x:170,y:32,t:"Email Account"},{x:276,y:168,t:"Phone Number"},{x:64,y:168,t:"Cloud Account"}] as n}
      
        
        {n.t}
      
    {/each}
    
      
      
      
    
    
      
      
      
    
    Circular dependency
    No account can be created first
  
  Illustrative cycle: email requires phone verification; phone provider requires a cloud account; cloud account requires email.


The agent cannot complete provisioning because every resource requires another resource that doesn't exist yet.
This is a classic dependency cycle.
2. Other cycles AgentPort could encounter



Cycle
What happens



Payment ↔ Account
Creating a payment account requires a paid service, but that service requires the payment account


Email ↔ Phone
Email registration requires phone verification; phone registration requires an email


Identity ↔ Provider
Provider requires verified agent identity; AgentPort verification requires an account with that provider


Credential ↔ Cloud
Credentials are stored in a cloud vault that requires the credentials to access


Recovery ↔ Account
Account A uses B for recovery, while B uses A for recovery


Some are hard blockers during initial creation. Others are dangerous because a failure in one account can make another impossible to recover.
3. How AgentPort should solve this
I would introduce a lightweight dependency graph into the account registry, rather than build an entire workflow engine.
Each account declares its dependencies and their purpose.
interface AccountDependency {
  accountId: string;
  dependsOn: string;

  type:
    | "provisioning"
    | "authentication"
    | "billing"
    | "recovery"
    | "runtime";
}

For provisioning, AgentPort should detect cycles before executing account-creation requests.

  **Interactive dependency checker**
  {@body const [emailPhone,setEmailPhone] = DIL.useState(true)}
  {@body const [phoneCloud,setPhoneCloud] = DIL.useState(true)}
  {@body const [cloudEmail,setCloudEmail] = DIL.useState(true)}
  {@body const cycle = emailPhone && phoneCloud && cloudEmail}
  
    Email requires phone
    Phone requires cloud account
    Cloud account requires email
  
  
  
    
    
      **{cycle?"Cycle detected":"No cycle in these dependencies"}**
      {cycle?"Provisioning is blocked until at least one dependency can be satisfied independently.":"The selected graph has no directed cycle, so an ordering can be determined."}
    
  
  {#if cycle}
    Possible resolution: use an existing owner-verified email, phone or cloud account as a bootstrap resource.
  {/if}


4. Bootstrap resources are the key
AgentPort should support bootstrap resources: credentials, accounts, or payment methods that already exist and can be used to establish new accounts.
For example:

  
    **Verified owner / existing payment method**
    Bootstrap trust and funding
  
  
  
    {#each ["Agent Email","Agent Phone"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    **New Service Accounts**
  


This works particularly well with your three account models:

Agent-native: the provider provisions the account directly.
User-parent: an existing authorized account bootstraps a child resource.
AgentPort-parent: an approved AgentPort master account bootstraps a child resource.

That means not every agent needs to create every account from scratch.
5. Don't reject every circular dependency
This is the subtle part.
A circular provisioning dependency is generally a blocker if none of the required resources already exists.
But a circular runtime dependency may be perfectly valid.
For example:

An email account sends notifications through a phone service.
The phone service sends alerts through the email account.

Both accounts can operate normally once they're established.
Similarly, two accounts can list each other as recovery contacts. That isn't necessarily a provisioning blocker, but it creates correlated recovery risk.
So AgentPort should distinguish:



Dependency type
Cycle policy



Provisioning
Detect and block unresolved cycles


Authentication
Detect potential login deadlocks


Billing
Flag funding loops and payment failure risk


Recovery
Warn about correlated lockout risk


Runtime
Usually allow, with loop protection where needed


6. How far should AgentPort go?
Given your goal of keeping AgentPort lightweight, I wouldn't build an autonomous dependency-planning engine inside it.
Instead, AgentPort should expose three capabilities:
dependencies.inspect — returns what an account needs.
dependencies.validate — checks whether a proposed provisioning plan has unresolved cycles.
dependencies.resolve — returns available bootstrap options or a suggested ordering, without autonomously executing everything.
Naelao or another agent runtime can then decide what to do.
The deeper insight is that AgentPort isn't only an identity registry; it could become a registry of relationships between agent-owned resources. That would let agents understand what they depend on, avoid provisioning deadlocks, and prevent accidental loss of access when an important account is suspended or deleted.


You:
What about more that one agents sharing an account


ChatGPT:
Yes. AgentPort should support multiple agents sharing one service account, and I think this should be a fundamental part of its account model.
In fact, an account shouldn't necessarily belong to one agent. It can belong to a human, organization, or AgentPort-managed parent, while multiple agents receive different permissions to use it.
The important architectural change is to separate account ownership from account access.
1. One account, multiple agents

  
    
    **Company WhatsApp Business Account**
    Owned by the company, registered in AgentPort
  
  
  
    **AgentPort Access Control**
    Permissions · Credentials · Audit · Event subscriptions
  
  
  
    {#each [{name:"Sales Agent",scope:"Send messages"},{name:"Support Agent",scope:"Read and reply"},{name:"Manager Agent",scope:"Manage templates"}] as a}
      
        
          
          {a.name}
          {a.scope}
        
      
    {/each}
  


All three agents use the same WhatsApp account, but they don't necessarily have the same permissions.
This also applies to Stripe, Coinbase wallets, cloud resources, email inboxes, SaaS accounts and browser sessions.
2. Different sharing models
I'd support four:



Model
Example



Exclusive
One agent has dedicated access to its own email


Shared
Three agents operate one company inbox


Delegated
An agent grants another agent limited access to a resource it controls, if authorized


Temporary
An agent receives access for one task or a limited period


An agent being assigned an account doesn't mean it has the right to share that account further. Delegation must be permitted by the actual owner and provider.
3. One account record, many access grants
Rather than copying the same account for every agent, AgentPort could store one account and multiple access grants.
interface ServiceAccount {
  id: string;
  providerId: string;
  externalAccountId: string;

  ownerType: "user" | "organization" | "agentport";
  ownerId: string;

  accountModel:
    | "agent_native"
    | "user_parent"
    | "agentport_parent";

  credentialRef: string;
}

interface AccountGrant {
  accountId: string;
  agentId: string;

  permissions: string[];
  expiresAt?: string;

  grantedBy: string;
}

For example:
{
  "accountId": "whatsapp_company_1",
  "grants": [
    {
      "agentId": "sales_agent",
      "permissions": ["messages.send"]
    },
    {
      "agentId": "support_agent",
      "permissions": [
        "messages.read",
        "messages.send"
      ]
    }
  ]
}

The credentials remain in AgentPort's vault or with the authorized provider connection. Agents receive scoped access through AgentPort rather than copies of the account's secrets.
4. What if multiple agents use the account simultaneously?
That's where sharing gets interesting.
Imagine two agents both receive a customer message through the same WhatsApp number.
Without coordination, both might respond.
AgentPort should distinguish permission to use an account from ownership of an incoming event.

  
    
    
      **Fan-out**

  Multiple agents receive the same event. Useful when a support agent handles the customer while an analytics agent records activity.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Competing consumers**

  One agent claims the event from a shared consumer group. Useful when several agents perform the same support role.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Filtered subscriptions**

  Sales events go to the sales agent, support events to the support agent, and billing events to the finance agent.
</box>

  


You already considered activity leases for AgentPort. They fit here: a lease can prevent two competing consumers from processing the same event simultaneously, while expiring automatically if a consumer crashes.
But a lease should be scoped to an event or consumer group, not globally lock the WhatsApp account. Other authorized agents may still need to send messages.
5. The account model becomes a graph
With shared accounts and dependencies, AgentPort's relationships might look like this:

  
    
    Organization
    
    Shared Service Account
    
      
      
      
      
    
    {#each [{x:58,t:"Sales Agent"},{x:170,t:"Support Agent"},{x:282,t:"Finance Agent"}] as a}
      
        
        {a.t}
      
    {/each}
    owns
    grants
  


An account can have:

One legal owner or clearly defined ownership arrangement.
One provider parent account, where applicable.
Multiple authorized agents.
Multiple dependent resources.
Multiple event subscriptions.
Separate billing and usage records.

This also helps with revocation: removing an agent's access doesn't delete the shared account or interrupt the other agents.
6. What about agent-native accounts?
Even an account created specifically for Agent A might be shareable with Agent B, if the provider permits delegation.
For example, Agent A has its own dedicated email address but lets Agent B send messages through it temporarily.
AgentPort could grant that permission without giving Agent B the mailbox password.
However, if a provider issues credentials that cannot be delegated or shared, AgentPort must respect that restriction.
And when a shared provider credential has broad privileges, AgentPort's internal permissions only protect actions mediated through AgentPort. They don't create provider-enforced isolation if an agent can obtain the raw credential.
My recommended rule
Accounts are resources. Agents are principals. Access grants connect them.
Don't build AgentPort around the assumption that every agent owns exactly one account or that every account serves exactly one agent.
Build it as a many-to-many relationship:
[\text{Agents};\longleftrightarrow;\text{Service Accounts}]
That supports dedicated accounts, shared company accounts, temporary delegation, parent-child provisioning and multi-agent collaboration under one consistent architecture.
It also means an organization could provision one WhatsApp number, one Stripe account and one cloud project, then let dozens of agents collaborate through them without duplicating credentials or paying for unnecessary accounts.

ChatGPT:
This Cloudflare announcement is very relevant to AgentPort, particularly your plans for agent identities, browser automation, and convincing online services to accept agents.
Cloudflare is starting to give website owners something AgentPort ultimately needs them to have: the ability to recognize AI agents and decide which ones to trust.
What Cloudflare announced
November 4, 2026 · 


Cloudflare introduced three important capabilities:

Agent visibility: Websites can see which supported personal AI assistants are visiting, their request volumes, paths, and errors.
Agent-specific policies: Website owners can allow or block individual supported assistants instead of treating all automated traffic identically.
Agent-to-user visibility: Through Account Abuse Protection, currently in early access, websites can see which personal assistants are associated with particular user accounts, including login and signup activity. 

This is a meaningful change from treating agents as anonymous bots.
Why this matters for AgentPort
Consider the relationship between your infrastructure and Cloudflare's announcement.

  
    
    **Naelao / OpenClaw / Other Agents**
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Persistent identity · Owner · Account relationships · Authorization
  
  
  
    **Cloudflare-protected Website**
    Recognize agent traffic · Apply access policies
  
  
  
    **Online Service**
    Decides whether to grant account access or agent provisioning
  


This is a potential future integration architecture, not something Cloudflare has announced as an AgentPort integration.
The critical distinction is that Cloudflare currently recognizes supported personal assistants at the traffic level. It does not automatically recognize every individual AgentPort identity or authorize those agents to create accounts.
The most interesting part of the article
Cloudflare explicitly identifies three areas it wants to work on next:

Carrying user context through an agent.
Helping websites assess agent reputation.
Establishing accountability when something goes wrong. 

Those align closely with the problems AgentPort is trying to solve.



Cloudflare's direction
Potential AgentPort contribution



Identify personal assistants
Persistent identity for individual agents


Associate agents with users
Verified ownership and delegation


Assess reputation
Agent history and verifiable trust signals


Establish accountability
Authorization and auditable activity


Control agent access
Provider-recognized permissions and revocation


But Cloudflare may develop some of these capabilities itself or partner with other identity providers. This announcement is both a potential opportunity and a competitive signal.
One strategic implication I'd pay attention to
Previously, we discussed convincing Amazon and other large services to create agent-native accounts.
This article suggests another possible path:
Before asking websites to create accounts for agents, help them recognize which agents are acting on behalf of which authorized users or organizations.
That is a smaller adoption step.
A website could initially recognize an agent, apply access policies, and let it use an existing customer account. Later, it could offer agent-specific credentials or subaccounts.
For AgentPort, that suggests keeping two distinct identities:

Agent identity: Which persistent agent is making the request?
Account authorization: Which user, company or agent-owned account permits this particular action?

This also fits your shared-account model: multiple agents can operate one company account while retaining distinct identities and audit trails.
What I would do now
I wouldn't rush to build a Cloudflare-specific connector or an entirely new reputation network.
I'd make sure AgentPort's identity records can eventually support verifiable ownership, delegation, credential rotation and revocation. Then investigate how Cloudflare identifies supported personal assistants and whether third-party agent operators can participate.
There's a significant limitation: browser traffic identification alone is not cryptographic proof of an individual agent's identity, and Cloudflare's allow policy is not permission to violate a website's account rules.
My main takeaway: Cloudflare is making agent access a first-class website security concern. That strengthens the case for AgentPort, but it also means the window to establish a useful independent agent identity layer may be competitive.
The particularly interesting possibility is AgentPort becoming an identity and authorization source that website security platforms can verify, rather than AgentPort having to negotiate a completely bespoke integration with every website.

ChatGPT:
This is a significant development for AgentPort.
The Instagram post from The Straits Times says Singapore is considering a national digital identity infrastructure for AI agents, including a registry that would let organizations verify an agent's identity and mandate.
The most interesting part is the emphasis on two requirements:

Every agent should be bound to a human or humans.
Agents should have verifiable identities and clear mandates when interacting across organizations.

That's remarkably close to the trust model we've been designing for AgentPort.
But there's an important distinction: the post describes a proposal raised in Singapore's political discussions, not confirmation that Singapore has launched a national agent identity system.
What I found in the actual parliamentary discussion
The proposal was made on November 4, 2026, by Singapore MP Tin Pei Ling.
She suggested a registry similar in concept to Singpass, where an agent's identity is linked to the responsible person, team or organization, and its authorization can be checked. Material changes to its mandate would require renewed authorization. 
Even more interesting, the Singapore government disclosed that:

GovTech is already developing an internal registry for government-deployed agents and their responsible officers.
IMDA is exploring DNS-based agent verification with Cloudflare, Google, GoDaddy and Infoblox.
Singapore's financial regulator is developing agent-transaction safeguards, including identity verification, mandate checks and auditability. <Cite refs={["turn729851search1","turn729851search4"]}/>

This goes beyond a politician simply suggesting an idea. Several relevant infrastructure efforts are already underway, although the national registry itself remains under consideration.
What this means for AgentPort

  
    
    **Verified human / organization**
    Ultimately accountable principal
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Identity · Mandates · Delegation · Revocation
  
  
  
    {#each ["Agent A","Agent B","Agent C"] as a}
      
        
          
          {a}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    **External services / institutions**
    Verify identity and authorization before granting access
  


Your existing architecture already anticipates much of this: persistent agent IDs, parent accounts, shared accounts, scoped grants and organization ownership.
The piece I would elevate is the verifiable mandate.
For example, an agent might present a signed statement proving:
{
  "agent_id": "agent_123",
  "issuer": "verified_company_456",
  "authorized_actions": [
    "read_orders",
    "manage_inventory"
  ],
  "expires_at": "2026-12-01T00:00:00Z"
}

A service could verify the signature and authorization status without trusting the agent's own claims. In practice, sensitive actions would still require audience restrictions, revocation checks and provider-side policy enforcement.
The strategic implication
I would not try to make AgentPort the national identity registry for AI agents.
Instead, design it to interoperate with national registries, enterprise identity providers and emerging verification standards.
That would let AgentPort remain useful whether an agent is registered through Naelao, an enterprise, or eventually a government-recognized identity system.
There is also a competitive warning: if governments and large infrastructure companies standardize identity verification, a proprietary AgentPort identity alone won't be enough of a moat.
Your differentiation could be what happens after verification: provisioning dedicated accounts, connecting parent/subaccounts, granting shared access and managing the agent's service relationships across providers.
The most important question this raises for AgentPort is whether it should become a trusted issuer of agent identities and mandates, or primarily an infrastructure layer that accepts identities and mandates issued by others.
I'd design for both, but prioritize accepting external trusted identities so that AgentPort doesn't depend on every institution recognizing its own registry.


ChatGPT:
What I found in the actual parliamentary discussion
The proposal was made on November 4, 2026, by Singapore MP Tin Pei Ling.
She suggested a registry similar in concept to Singpass, where an agent's identity is linked to the responsible person, team or organization, and its authorization can be checked. Material changes to its mandate would require renewed authorization. 
Even more interesting, the Singapore government disclosed that:

GovTech is already developing an internal registry for government-deployed agents and their responsible officers.
IMDA is exploring DNS-based agent verification with Cloudflare, Google, GoDaddy and Infoblox.
Singapore's financial regulator is developing agent-transaction safeguards, including identity verification, mandate checks and auditability. <Cite refs={["turn729851search1","turn729851search4"]}/>

This goes beyond a politician simply suggesting an idea. Several relevant infrastructure efforts are already underway, although the national registry itself remains under consideration.
What this means for AgentPort

  
    
    **Verified human / organization**
    Ultimately accountable principal
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Identity · Mandates · Delegation · Revocation
  
  
  
    {#each ["Agent A","Agent B","Agent C"] as a}
      
        
          
          {a}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    **External services / institutions**
    Verify identity and authorization before granting access
  


Your existing architecture already anticipates much of this: persistent agent IDs, parent accounts, shared accounts, scoped grants and organization ownership.
The piece I would elevate is the verifiable mandate.
For example, an agent might present a signed statement proving:
{
  "agent_id": "agent_123",
  "issuer": "verified_company_456",
  "authorized_actions": [
    "read_orders",
    "manage_inventory"
  ],
  "expires_at": "2026-12-01T00:00:00Z"
}

A service could verify the signature and authorization status without trusting the agent's own claims. In practice, sensitive actions would still require audience restrictions, revocation checks and provider-side policy enforcement.
The strategic implication
I would not try to make AgentPort the national identity registry for AI agents.
Instead, design it to interoperate with national registries, enterprise identity providers and emerging verification standards.
That would let AgentPort remain useful whether an agent is registered through Naelao, an enterprise, or eventually a government-recognized identity system.
There is also a competitive warning: if governments and large infrastructure companies standardize identity verification, a proprietary AgentPort identity alone won't be enough of a moat.
Your differentiation could be what happens after verification: provisioning dedicated accounts, connecting parent/subaccounts, granting shared access and managing the agent's service relationships across providers.
The most important question this raises for AgentPort is whether it should become a trusted issuer of agent identities and mandates, or primarily an infrastructure layer that accepts identities and mandates issued by others.
I'd design for both, but prioritize accepting external trusted identities so that AgentPort doesn't depend on every institution recognizing its own registry.

ChatGPT:
I think you're identifying a bigger opportunity for AgentPort than simply creating accounts for AI agents.
What if an agent could carry its identity, qualifications, reputation, and verified history between organizations without having to start from zero every time?
That's where blockchain could become useful. But I would separate portable identity from blockchain, because we can achieve much of the first without requiring the second.
1. The problem with copying human identity systems
Today, a human might need to prove the same facts repeatedly:

University verifies qualifications.
Employer verifies qualifications again.
Bank verifies identity.
Another employer repeats background checks.
Government institutions maintain separate records.

Some repeated checks are legally necessary, but much of the duplication comes from institutions not having a common way to trust previously issued evidence.
If AI agents end up with the same fragmented model, we could have:

  
    
    **One AI Agent**
  
  
  
    {#each ["Government Registry","Bank Identity","Cloud Provider ID","Company Registry"] as x}
      
        
          
          {x}
          Verify from scratch
        
      
    {/each}
  
  Four independent identity processes for the same agent.


This is exactly the kind of fragmentation AgentPort should avoid.
2. Instead: one portable identity, many trusted attestations
Imagine an agent has a persistent identity and accumulates verifiable credentials throughout its existence.

  
    
    **Agent Identity**
    Portable identity controlled by its authorized owner
  
  
  
    Agent Credential Portfolio
    {#each [{i:"shield-check",t:"Identity verified",s:"Issued by trusted identity provider"},{i:"graduation-cap",t:"Certified capability",s:"Issued by recognized evaluator"},{i:"briefcase-business",t:"Employment history",s:"Attested by previous organization"},{i:"check-check",t:"Operational track record",s:"Signed by participating services"}] as a}
      
        
        
          {a.t}
          {a.s}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  
  
    {#each ["Company A","Company B","Institution C"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
          Verify evidence
        
      
    {/each}
  


The new institution doesn't have to ask the agent to recreate its history.
It can verify previously issued credentials cryptographically, check whether they are still valid, and decide whether to accept them.
That's a much better model than having AgentPort, Singapore, Amazon and every bank each maintain completely unrelated identities.
3. Where blockchain fits
Blockchain could provide a shared trust anchor, but it doesn't need to store every agent's personal information or history.
I'd compare three approaches:



Architecture
Advantage
Limitation



Central AgentPort registry
Simple, fast, inexpensive
Depends heavily on AgentPort


Federated identities and signed credentials
Portable, no universal database required
Institutions still need trust policies


Blockchain-anchored identity
Shared verification and tamper-evident history
Privacy, governance, fees and key recovery complexity


My preference would be federated, cryptographically verifiable identity first, with optional blockchain anchoring later.
You could use concepts such as W3C Verifiable Credentials and Decentralized Identifiers without putting every operation onchain.
A signed credential can be verified offline in many situations. Blockchain becomes more useful when you need a shared, independently governed registry of issuers, revocation states, or other trust anchors.
4. An agent could have a portable professional history
This is where your employment analogy becomes especially interesting.
Imagine an AI accounting agent:

  
    
      
    
    
      **Accounting Agent #4821**
      Portable professional credentials
    
  
  
  
    
      **Created by Company A**

  Company A establishes the agent's identity and signs its initial mandate.
</list-item>
<list-item>
  **Completes 20,000 reconciliations**

  An independent evaluator attests to specific performance metrics and testing conditions.
</list-item>
<list-item>
  **Receives a capability certification**

  A recognized evaluator signs a credential that other organizations can verify.
</list-item>
<list-item>
  **Moves to Company B**

  Company B verifies the existing credentials, then issues its own employment mandate and permissions.
</list-item>

  


Company B doesn't need to repeat every capability test.
But it still needs to decide whether the agent is suitable for its particular work and grant its own permissions.
There is another subtle issue: an agent's identity is not the same as its model weights or accumulated memory.
If Company A owns the agent's proprietary code, fine-tuned weights or confidential knowledge, the agent can't automatically take those assets to Company B.
A portable credential could prove the agent previously performed certain work without transferring the underlying confidential data.
5. The hard part isn't the blockchain. It's trust.
Suppose AgentPort records:

"Agent 4821 has completed 100,000 successful financial transactions."

Who verified that?
Was it the agent itself? Its owner? A bank? An independent auditor?
A blockchain can make a false statement permanently tamper-evident. It cannot magically make that statement true.
So AgentPort needs to distinguish:

Identity: Which agent is this?
Issuer: Who made the claim?
Credential: What exactly was verified?
Mandate: What is the agent currently authorized to do?
Reputation: What evidence exists about its past behavior?
Trust decision: Does this particular institution accept that evidence?

This also prevents a dangerous situation where an agent builds an impressive reputation in one domain and is automatically trusted to perform unrelated high-risk tasks.
6. One more issue: agents can be copied
Unlike a human, an AI agent can be duplicated, modified, or redeployed.
Imagine an agent earns an excellent reputation. Someone copies its code and claims to be the same agent.
A portable identity system must bind the identity to controlled cryptographic keys and authorized operators, not merely a model name or code hash.
And if the agent's weights, tools or autonomy change substantially, previously issued capability certifications may no longer be valid.
I'd support:
interface AgentIdentity {
  id: string;
  controller: string;
  publicKeys: string[];
  credentials: string[];
  softwareVersion?: string;
  revocationStatus: string;
}

Credentials can then specify whether they apply to a particular software version, deployment, organization or agent lineage.
7. What I'd do for AgentPort
I'd build toward this architecture:

  
    **Portable Agent Identity**
    Cryptographic keys and verifiable credentials
  
  
  
    **AgentPort**
    Credential wallet · Verification · Mandates · Account provisioning
  
  
  
    {#each ["Enterprise Identity","Government Registry","Independent Issuers","Blockchain Registry"] as x}
      
        
          {x}
        
      
    {/each}
  
  External issuers and trust anchors are interoperable; no single registry must own every identity.


Initially, AgentPort can issue and verify its own signed identities and mandates.
Later, it can accept credentials issued by enterprises, governments, and independent verification organizations.
Eventually, a decentralized trust registry could make verification less dependent on AgentPort itself.
The bigger vision is that AgentPort doesn't need to become the central authority for all agents. It could become the infrastructure through which agents carry and use their identities, credentials, permissions, accounts and verifiable histories across the internet.
That is more ambitious than an agent account provider—and potentially much more defensible.
The design principle I'd adopt now is: an agent's identity and credentials should outlive its current runtime, hosting provider, and employer, while its access permissions remain controlled by whoever owns the relevant resources.
